\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
when in negation normal form, then it is
(syntactic) safety formula

(
Sistla, 1994
)
.

We define the
language of infinite sequences represented by a formula
\varphi\in\mathit{RP{-}LTL}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}\cup\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
as

\mathcal{L}(\varphi):=\{\rho\in{(\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}))}^{\omega}\mid\rho\models\varphi\}
.
If
\mathcal{L}(\varphi)=\emptyset
we say that
\varphi
is
unsatisfiable
, if
\mathcal{L}(\varphi)=(\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}))^{\omega}
we say that
\varphi
is
valid
, and if
\mathcal{L}(\varphi)\not=\emptyset
, we say that
\varphi
is
satisfiable
.

\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
Expressivity.

As
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
formulas allow us to relate values over time by expressing relationships between
\mathbb{X}
and
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
, it is possible to use the variables from
\mathbb{X}
as registers. Hence, in a sufficiently expressive theory domain, e.g. linear integer arithmetic, it is straightforward to encode computation formalisms like e.g. WHILE-programs or counter machines. Furthermore, since
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
formulas are interpreted over infinite sequences, it is easily possible to express properties like e.g. a program terminates (or reaches a given state) on all inputs.

If the atomic formulas are allowed to use
<
and
=
, and the variables in
\mathbb{X}
range over the integers, there exist
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
formulas
\varphi
such that
\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
is not
\omega
-regular, which follows from the result for constraint LTL established in
(
Demri and D’Souza, 2007
)
.
On the other hand,
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
generalizes LTL on the assertion level, and LTL does not capture all
\omega
-regular languages.
Thus, to specify in
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
an arbitrary
\omega
-regular property represented by an automaton on infinite words, we need, in general, to use additional variables to encode the states of the automaton and its transition relation.
The standard way to increase the expressive power of LTL (or LTL modulo theories) is via combination with regular expressions
(
Eisner and Fisman, 2006
)
(or regular expressions modulo theories
(
Veanes et al., 2023
)
), which capture better the higher-level declarative aspects of specifications.
While such an extension of
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
is worth investigating in the future, in this paper we focus on
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
, as it lifts LTL, which underlies the defacto standard specification language for reactive synthesis
(
Jacobs et al., 2023
)
.

\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
Realizability.

The core notion in reactive synthesis is
realizability
.
Intuitively, a temporal formula is realizable, if there exists a system that can react to any input from the environment over infinitely many rounds, such that the resulting infinite execution satisfies the formula.
We can formalize this as the statement that there
exists a function
mapping the initial assignment to
\mathbb{X}
and the sequence of inputs so far to the next assignment to the program variables
\mathbb{X}
, such that all possible sequences induced by this function satisfy the formula.
Formally, we define the notion of
realizability
of an
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
formula
\varphi
as follows.

Definition 4.4 (
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
Realizability).

A formula
\varphi\in\mathit{RP{-}LTL}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}\cup\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
is called
realizable
if and only if there exists a function

\sigma:\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X})\times\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{I})^{+}\to\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X})
such that for every infinite sequence
\mathit{input}\in\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{I})^{\omega}
of assignments to
\mathbb{I}
and initial assignment
\mathit{init}\in\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X})
, for the infinite word
\rho\in{(\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}))}^{\omega}
defined as

\rho[0]:=\mathit{init}\uplus\mathit{input}[0]
and
\rho[n]:=\sigma(\mathit{init},\mathit{input}[0,n-1])\uplus\mathit{input}[n]
for
n>0
,
\rho\models\varphi
holds.

Realizability and Synthesis Problem from
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}

The
realizability problem
is checking whether a formula
\Phi\in\mathit{RP{-}LTL}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}\cup\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
is realizable.
The
synthesis problem
is finding a witness for the realizing function (i.e. the system).

One way of representing this witness is as
reactive program

(
Heim and Dimitrova, 2024
)
.
Intuitively, this is a program that reads the inputs
\mathbb{I}
and updates its program variables (a superset of
\mathbb{X}
), repeating this indefinitely.

4.2.
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
Realizability Checking via Symbolic Game Solving

In the standard automata-theoretic approach to the synthesis of finite-state reactive systems from LTL specifications
(
Luttenberger et al., 2020
)
, the specification is translated to a deterministic parity automaton that is interpreted as a two-player turn-based game between the synthesized system and its environment.
A winning strategy for the system player from a dedicated initial vertex corresponds to a finite-state implementation that realizes the specification. If, on the other hand, the environment wins from the initial vertex, the specification is unrealizable.

To perform realizability checking and synthesis from
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
specifications, we can adopt a similar flow.
The key difference is that instead of Boolean atomic propositions,

\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
formulas are over variables with possibly infinite domains.
To address this, we translate
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
specifications to deterministic parity automata with
symbolically represented transition functions
, which are then interpreted as
symbolic game structures
.
In that way, we reduce the realizability checking and synthesis problems for
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
to the problem of solving a symbolically represented infinite-state game.
Next in this section, we describe this reduction in detail, after which discuss its fundamental limitation, which our proposed approach addresses as presented in the rest of the paper.

4.2.1.
Constructing Automata from
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
Formulas

We now explain the first step, which is the translation of
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
formulas to automata.
We remark that this translation is separate from and independent of the monitor construction that we present in subsequent sections.
Here, we utilize the respective construction for LTL specifications. To this end, we interpret the given
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
formula
\varphi
as an LTL formula
\widehat{\varphi}
by replacing the atomic sub-formulas elements of
\mathit{QF}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}\cup\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
by Boolean propositions.
More concretely, we construct the LTL formula
\widehat{\varphi}
from
\varphi
by replacing each atomic formula in
\mathit{Atoms}(\varphi)
with a unique propositional variable.
Then, we obtain a DPA
\mathcal{A}_{\widehat{\varphi}}
with alphabet
2^{\mathit{AP}(\widehat{\varphi})}
, where
\mathit{AP}(\widehat{\varphi})
are the atomic propositions in
\widehat{\varphi}
.
The language of
\mathcal{A}_{\widehat{\varphi}}
is
\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\varphi})
.
In practice, this can be done using standard methods such as those implemented in
(
Duret-Lutz et al., 2022
;
Duret-Lutz et al., 2016
)
.

Intuitively, by replacing back the propositions from
\mathit{AP}(\widehat{\varphi})
by the original atomic formulas
\mathit{Atoms}(\varphi)
in the transition function of the DPA
\mathcal{A}_{\widehat{\varphi}}
, we can obtain a symbolic DPA representing the language
\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
.
In the next subsection we use this idea to directly interpret
\mathcal{A}_{\widehat{\varphi}}
as a
symbolically represented
two-player graph game encoding the realizability problem for
\varphi
.

4.2.2.
Symbolic Infinite-State Games for
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
Realizability and Synthesis

As we explained above, due to the possibly infinite domains of the variables in
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
specifications, the games encoding the synthesis problem have in general an infinite set of vertices.
To this end, we represent two-player game graphs via
symbolic game structures
defined next.

Definition 4.5 (Symbolic Game Structure).

A
symbolic game structure
is a tuple
(L,l_{\mathit{init}},\mathbb{I},\mathbb{X},\mathit{dom},\delta)
where

L
is a finite set of
locations,

l_{\mathit{init}}\in L
is the
initial location
,

\mathbb{I}\subseteq\mathit{Vars}
is a finite set of
input variables
,

\mathbb{X}\subseteq\mathit{Vars}
is a finite set of
program variables
,

\mathit{dom}:L\mapsto\mathit{QF}(\mathbb{X})
is the
domain of the states
,
and
\delta:L\times L\mapsto\mathit{QF}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}\cup\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
is the
transition relation
.

\delta
is required to be
deterministic
and
non-blocking
, i.e., for all
l\in L
and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X})
where
\mathbf{x}\models_{T}\mathit{dom}(l)
we have that

(1)

for every input assignment
\mathbf{i}\in\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{I})
and program variable assignment
\mathbf{v}\in\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X})
, there exists
at most one
location
l^{\prime}\in L
such that
\mathbf{x}\uplus\mathbf{i}\uplus\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\models_{T}\delta(l,l^{\prime})
.

(2)

there exist
\mathbf{i}\in\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{I})
,
\mathbf{v}\in\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X})
and
l^{\prime}\in L
such that
\mathbf{v}\models_{T}\mathit{dom}(l^{\prime})
and
\mathbf{x}\uplus\mathbf{i}\uplus\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\models_{T}\delta(l,l^{\prime})
.

Intuitively, a symbolic game structure
\mathcal{G}=(L,l_{\mathit{init}},\mathbb{I},\mathbb{X},\mathit{dom},\delta)
represents a two-player game graph encoding the interaction between an environment selecting values for the input variables
\mathbb{I}
and a system selecting the next values of the program variables
\mathbb{X}
.
The function
\mathit{dom}
constrains the values of the program variables to a specific domain (represented symbolically via formulas in
\mathit{QF}(\mathbb{X})
), and the transition relation
\delta
constrains the choices of the two players.
From a state
(l,\mathbf{x})\in L\times\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X})
with
\mathbf{x}\models_{T}\mathit{dom}(l)
, the environment selects an input
\mathbf{i}\in\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{I})
and the system selects a next assignment
\mathbf{v}\in\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X})
to the program variables such that for some
l^{\prime}\in L
it holds that
\mathbf{v}\models_{T}\mathit{dom}(l^{\prime})
and
\mathbf{x}\uplus\mathbf{i}\uplus\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\models_{T}\delta(l,l^{\prime})
. Then, the game proceeds to the new state
(l^{\prime},\mathbf{v})
and the process repeats from there, resulting in an infinite sequence of states.

The semantics of a symbolic game structure
\mathcal{G}
, which is a possibly infinite two-player turn-based game graph, is formalized in the next definition.

Definition 4.6 (Semantics of Symbolic Game Structures).

The semantics of a symbolic game structure

\mathcal{G}=(L,l_{\mathit{init}},\mathbb{I},\mathbb{X},\mathit{dom},\delta)
is the game graph

\llbracket\mathcal{G}\rrbracket=(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S}_{\mathit{Env}},\mathcal{S}_{\mathit{Sys}},\tau)
where

\mathcal{S}:=\mathcal{S}_{\mathit{Env}}\uplus\mathcal{S}_{\mathit{Sys}}
,

•

\mathcal{S}_{\mathit{Env}}:=\{(l,\mathbf{x})\in L\times\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X})\mid\mathbf{x}\models_{T}\mathit{dom}(l)\}
;

•

\mathcal{S}_{\mathit{Sys}}:=\{((l,\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{i})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathit{Env}}\times\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{I})\mid\exists l^{\prime}\in L\exists\mathbf{v}\in\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X}).\mathbf{v}\models_{T}\mathit{dom}(l^{\prime})\text{ and }\newline
\phantom{((l,\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{i})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathit{Env}}\times\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{I})\mid\exists l^{\prime}\in L\exists\nu\in\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X})\qquad}\mathbf{x}\uplus\mathbf{i}\uplus\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\models_{T}\delta(l,l^{\prime})\}
;

•

\tau\subseteq(\mathcal{S}_{\mathit{Env}}\times\mathcal{S}_{\mathit{Sys}})\cup(\mathcal{S}_{\mathit{Sys}}\times\mathcal{S}_{\mathit{Env}})
is the smallest relation such that

–

(s,(s,\mathbf{i}))\in\tau
for every
s\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathit{Env}}
and
\mathbf{i}\in\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{I})
such that
(s,\mathbf{i})\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathit{Sys}}
,

–

(((l,\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{i}),(l^{\prime},\mathbf{v}))\in\tau
if and only if
\mathbf{x}\uplus\mathbf{i}\uplus\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\models_{T}\delta(l,l^{\prime})
.

For symbolic game structures, we consider winning conditions defined in terms of the infinite sequence of locations visited in a play, which we refer to as
location-based winning conditions
.
Such winning conditions are independent of the valuations of the inputs and program variables, and, as we will see later in this section, are sufficient for encoding the
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
synthesis problem.

Definition 4.7 (Location-Based Winning Condition).

A
location-based wining condition
in a symbolic game structure
\mathcal{G}=(L,l_{\mathit{init}},\mathbb{I},\mathbb{X},\mathit{dom},\delta)
is a set of
infinite sequences of locations
\Lambda\subseteq L^{\omega}
.
A
location-based parity condition
is defined by a coloring function
\lambda:L\to\mathbb{N}
such that

\mathit{Parity}(\mathcal{G},\lambda)=\{l_{0}l_{1}\ldots\in L^{\omega}\mid\text{ the highest number occurring infinitely often in }\lambda(l_{0})\lambda(l_{1})\ldots\text{ is even}\}
.

For a play
\xi
of
\llbracket\mathcal{G}\rrbracket
of the form

(l_{0},\mathbf{x}_{0})((l_{0},\mathbf{x}_{0}),\mathbf{i}_{0})(l_{1},\mathbf{x}_{1})((l_{1},\mathbf{x}_{1}),\mathbf{i}_{1}),\ldots\in(\mathcal{S}_{\mathit{Env}}\cdot\mathcal{S}_{\mathit{Sys}})^{\omega}
or of the form

((l_{0},\mathbf{x}_{0}),\mathbf{i}_{0})(l_{1},\mathbf{x}_{1})((l_{1},\mathbf{x}_{1}),\mathbf{i}_{1}),\ldots\in(\mathcal{S}_{\mathit{Sys}}\cdot\mathcal{S}_{\mathit{Env}})^{\omega}
we define
\mathit{Loc}(\xi):=l_{0}l_{1}l_{2}\ldots\in L^{\omega}
to be the corresponding sequence of game locations visited by
\xi
.
A location-based winning condition
\Lambda
for
\mathcal{G}
defines a winning condition
\llbracket\Lambda\rrbracket\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{\omega}
where

\llbracket\Lambda\rrbracket:=\{\xi\in\mathcal{S}^{\omega}\mid\mathit{Loc}(\xi)\in\Lambda\}
.
A
symbolic game
is a pair
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
of
a symbolic game structure
\mathcal{G}
and
a location-based winning condition
\Lambda
.

We are now ready to describe the construction of a symbolic game structure equipped with a location-based winning condition for a given
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
formula
\varphi\in\mathit{RP{-}LTL}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}\cup\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{\widehat{\varphi}}=(Q,\Sigma,q_{0},\delta,\lambda)
be a DPA with

\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{\widehat{\varphi}})=\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\varphi})
constructed from

\varphi
as explained in
Section

4.2.1
.
From
\mathcal{A}_{\widehat{\varphi}}
we construct the symbolic game structure

\mathcal{G}_{\varphi}=(Q,q_{0},\mathbb{I},\mathbb{X},\mathit{dom},\delta_{\mathcal{G}})
where

•

the set of locations is the set of automaton states
Q
, and
\mathit{dom}(q)=\top
for all
q\in Q
, and

•

the transition relation
\delta_{\mathcal{G}}
is defined based on
\delta
such that for all
q,q^{\prime}\in Q
,

\delta_{\mathcal{G}}(q,q^{\prime}):=\bigvee_{\{a\in\Sigma\mid\delta(q,a)=q^{\prime}\}}\Big(\bigwedge_{\{\alpha\in\mathit{Atoms}(\varphi)\mid\widehat{\alpha}\in a\}}\alpha\land\bigwedge_{\{\alpha\in\mathit{Atoms}(\varphi)\mid\widehat{\alpha}\not\in a\}}\neg\alpha\Big).

Intuitively, the formula
\delta_{\mathcal{G}}(q,q^{\prime})\in\mathit{QF}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}\cup\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
is defined as the disjunction over all letters
a\in 2^{\mathit{AP}(\widehat{\varphi})}
that label a transition for
q
to
q^{\prime}
. Each such
a
corresponds to the conjunction of the propositions that belong to
a
and the negation of those that do not. Replacing each such proposition with the original formula in
\mathit{QF}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}\cup\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
gives us the formula
\delta_{\mathcal{G}}(q,q^{\prime})
representing the assignments to
\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}\cup\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
with which the game transitions form
q
to
q^{\prime}
.

Since the automaton
\mathcal{A}_{\widehat{\varphi}}
with alphabet
\Sigma=2^{\mathit{AP}(\widehat{\varphi})}
is deterministic, and its transition function
\delta
is total, the definition of
\delta_{\mathcal{G}}
guarantees that
\mathcal{G}_{\varphi}
satisfies the conditions of
Definition

4.5
.

The coloring function
\lambda
of
\mathcal{A}_{\widehat{\varphi}}
yields a location-based parity winning condition
\mathit{Parity}(\mathcal{G}_{\varphi},\lambda)
.

We say that
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
is a
symbolic game for the
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
specification
\varphi
if and only if for some DPA

\mathcal{A}_{\widehat{\varphi}}=(Q,\Sigma,q_{0},\delta,\lambda)
with
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{\widehat{\varphi}})=\mathcal{L}(\widehat{\varphi})
,

\mathcal{G}
is the symbolic game structure obtained from
\mathcal{A}_{\widehat{\varphi}}
as defined above, and

\Lambda=\mathit{Parity}(\mathcal{G},\lambda)

The next theorem establishes that the symbolic game structure
\mathcal{G}_{\varphi}
equipped with the winning condition
\mathit{Parity}(\mathcal{G}_{\varphi},\lambda)
encodes the
realizability problem for
\varphi
.

Theorem 4.8 (Symbolic Game Correctness).

Let
\varphi\in\mathit{RP{-}LTL}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}\cup\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
be a formula
and
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
be a symbolic game for
\varphi
with

\mathcal{G}=(L,l_{\mathit{init}},\mathbb{I},\mathbb{X},\mathit{dom},\delta)
.
The formula
\varphi
is realizable if and only if
(l_{\mathit{init}},\mathbf{x})\in\mathit{Win}_{\mathit{Sys}}(\llbracket\mathcal{G}\rrbracket,\llbracket\Lambda\rrbracket)
for every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X})
with
\mathbf{x}\models_{T}\mathit{dom}(l_{\mathit{init}})
.

Theorem

4.8
gives us a method for reducing the realizability and synthesis problems for
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
to the solving of an infinite-state game and the computation of a winning strategy for the system.
In contrast to the automata-theoretic approach to synthesis from LTL, where the construction of the deterministic automaton is often the bottleneck, here
the result of the translation is an infinite-state two-player game, the problem of solving which is in general undecidable.

5.
Augmenting the Synthesis Game with a Monitor for
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}

The procedure outlined in
Section

4.2.2
for translating an
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
formula
\varphi
into a symbolic game, does not account for the first-order concretization of the propositions in the propositional formula
\widehat{\varphi}
. In particular, any state-space reductions applied by the underlying automata construction are not able to take advantage of the semantics of the propositions.
Since techniques for solving infinite-state games face significant challenges, applying possible simplifications to the game during its construction is essential.
Following the automata construction, the
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
formula is forgotten, with the deterministic automaton being its low-level representation.
Thus, in order to take advantage of the structure of the
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
formula it is desirable to
apply semantic simplifications at the formula level and during the construction of the synthesis game
.

In this paper, we present a principled, generally applicable approach to perform such semantic simplifications. The key idea is to construct a so-called
monitor for the given
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
formula

\varphi
that can be used to augment the constructed game with semantic information. This augmentation, which will be in the form of constructing the product of the monitor and the game, will perform game-structure reductions based on this semantic information.
More concretely, the role of the monitor is to identify states of the automaton
\mathcal{A}_{\widehat{\varphi}}
whose language is empty, or contains all possible executions, thus identifying unsatisfiable or vacuous requirements as illustrated in
Section

2
.

Remark

The symbolic game is constructed from a DPA for the propositional formula
\widehat{\varphi}
. Currently, there exist no techniques and tools for
directly
constructing
deterministic symbolic automata
from temporal logic specifications over non-Boolean domains.
We note that the approach proposed here and the monitor construction presented in the next subsection are independent from the procedure used to construct the DPA and the resulting symbolic game.

5.1.
Monitors for
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
Formulas

We now proceed with the formal definition of our notion of
monitor for
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
formulas
.

Definition 5.1 (Monitor).

A
monitor
over
\mathbb{X}
,
\mathbb{I}
,
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
is a tuple
M=(Q,q_{\mathit{init}},\mathcal{P},\delta,\mathit{Verdict})
where

•

Q
is a finite set of
monitor states
and
q_{\mathit{init}}
is the
monitor’s initial state
,

•

\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathit{QF}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}\cup\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
is a finite set of
monitored predicates
,

•

\delta:Q\times 2^{\mathcal{P}}\to Q
is the monitor’s
transition function
,

•

\mathit{Verdict}:Q\to\{\mathsf{UNSAT},\mathsf{SAFETY},\mathsf{OPEN}\}
is a
verdict-labelling function
such that for every
q,q^{\prime}\in Q
where
\delta(q,a)=q^{\prime}
for some
a
, the following two conditions are satisfied:

–

if
\mathit{Verdict}(q)=\mathsf{UNSAT}
, then
\mathit{Verdict}(q^{\prime})=\mathsf{UNSAT}
, and

–

if
\mathit{Verdict}(q)=\mathsf{SAFETY}
, then
\mathit{Verdict}(q^{\prime})\in\{\mathsf{UNSAT},\mathsf{SAFETY}\}
.

Thus, a monitor is a finite-state automaton whose transitions are labelled with sets of predicates, that is, formulas in
\mathit{QF}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}\cup\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
. Each such set defines a subset of
\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}\cup\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
which determines the variable assignments with which this transition can be taken.
We define a function
\delta^{*}_{M}:(\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}))^{*}\to Q
that maps each finite sequence
\pi\in(\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}))^{*}
to the state that
M
reaches after reading
\pi
.
Formally, we let

\delta^{*}_{M}(\pi)=q_{\mathit{init}}
if
|\pi|=0
or
|\pi|=1
, and otherwise we let

\delta^{*}_{M}(\pi)=q
if
\pi=\pi^{\prime}\cdot\nu_{1}\cdot\nu_{2}
,

\delta^{*}_{M}(\pi^{\prime}\cdot\nu_{1})=q^{\prime}
and

q:=\delta(q^{\prime},\{p\in\mathcal{P}\mid\langle\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\rangle\models p\})
.

The verdict-labelling function
\mathit{Verdict}
of a monitor
M
maps each of the monitor’s states to an element of the set
\{\mathsf{UNSAT},\mathsf{SAFETY},\mathsf{OPEN}\}
.
Intuitively, in order for
M
to be a monitor for a
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
formula
\varphi
, we require that for each finite prefix
\pi\in(\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}))^{*}
, the verdict assigned to the state
\delta^{*}_{M}(\pi)
that
M
reaches when reading
\pi
should be consistent with the language
\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
. This requirement is formalized in the next definition.

Definition 5.2 (Monitor for an
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
Formula).

Let
\varphi\in\mathit{RP{-}LTL}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}\cup\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
be a formula.
A monitor
M=(Q,q_{\mathit{init}},\mathcal{P},\delta,\mathit{Verdict})
over variables
\mathbb{X}
,
\mathbb{I}
,
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
is a
monitor for
\varphi
iff for every
\pi\in(\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}))^{*}
and
\nu\in\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I})
, the following properties hold for
q=\delta^{*}_{M}(\pi\cdot\nu)
:

(1)

if
\mathit{Verdict}(q)=\mathsf{UNSAT}
, then
\pi\cdot\nu\cdot\rho\not\in\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
for all
\rho\in(\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}))^{\omega}
;

(2)

if
\mathit{Verdict}(q)=\mathsf{SAFETY}
, then, for every
\rho\in(\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}))^{\omega}
,

if
\mathit{Verdict}(\delta^{*}_{M}(\pi\cdot\nu\cdot\rho[0,i]))\neq\mathsf{UNSAT}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, then it holds that
\pi\cdot\nu\cdot\rho\in\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
.

Thus, in particular, a monitor
M
for an
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
formula
\varphi
ensures that the verdict assigned to a finite sequence
\pi\in(\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}))^{*}
, that is
\mathit{Verdict}(\delta^{*}_{M}(\pi))
, is
\mathsf{UNSAT}
only if the language
\{\rho\in(\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}))^{\omega}\mid\pi\cdot\rho\in\mathcal{L}(\varphi)\}
is empty.

Intuitively,
\mathsf{UNSAT}
verdicts allow us to identify unsatisfiable requirements, while
\mathsf{SAFETY}
verdicts are useful for the simplification of the winning condition of the game resulting from the augmentation of the symbolic game structure
\mathcal{G}_{\varphi}
with a monitor for
\varphi
.

Remark

The verdicts assigned by a monitor for an
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
formula
\varphi
are determined based on the language
\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
, and not based on the existence of strategies in the synthesis game. Hence, we speak of (un)satisfiability instead of (un)realizability when referring to the monitor’s role.
The reason for that is that the game-based interaction between the system and the environment is handled when solving the resulting game, while the utilization of the monitor is during the game’s construction.
The role of the monitor is to identify inconsistencies or implied sub-specifications by temporal and first-order reasoning on the formula level.
In
Section

6
we present our main technical contribution, which is a technique for the construction of monitors from
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
formulas.
Before that, we describe how a monitor for an
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
formula
\varphi
can be used to
prune a symbolic game for
\varphi
.

5.2.
Product of a Symbolic Game and a Monitor

We use a monitor for an
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
formula
\varphi
to prune a symbolic game for
\varphi
by constructing a product between the game and the monitor such that they both “run in parallel”.
This gives us a general method for adding semantic information to the resulting synthesis game, which is independent of how the symbolic game for
\varphi
was constructed, and of the concrete construction of the monitor, as long as it is a monitor for
\varphi
according to
Definition

5.2
.
In this construction, we use the verdict-labelling function of the monitor to modify the winning condition of the game.
For example, if the monitor assigns verdict
\mathsf{UNSAT}
, the respective product state is losing for the system player.
Furthermore, if the assigned verdict is
\mathsf{SAFETY}
, the winning condition for the respective sub-game can be switched to a safety winning condition.
This pruning has two advantages:
First, we disregard obvious bad choices for one of the players if they lead to a verdict that can already be computed from the monitor.
Second, for
\mathsf{SAFETY}
verdicts the winning condition simplifies from e.g. a parity condition to a safety condition, which can potentially make solving the game easier.
Formally, we define this product as follows:

Definition 5.3 (Game-Monitor Product).

Let

\mathcal{G}=(L,l_{\mathit{init}},\mathbb{I},\mathbb{X},\mathit{dom},\delta)
be a symbolic game structure,

\Lambda\subseteq L^{\omega}
be a location-based winning condition,
and let
M=(Q,q_{\mathit{init}},\mathcal{P},\delta_{M},\mathit{Verdict})
be a monitor over

\mathbb{X}
,
\mathbb{I}
,
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
.
We define the
game-monitor product
for
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
and
M
as the pair
(\mathcal{G}_{\times},\Lambda_{\times})
consisting of the symbolic game structure

\mathcal{G}_{\times}:=(L\times Q,(l_{\mathit{init}},q_{\mathit{init}}),\mathbb{I},\mathbb{X},\mathit{dom}_{\times},\delta_{\times})
and the location-based winning condition
\Lambda_{\times}\subseteq{(L\times Q)}^{\omega}
where

•

\mathit{dom}_{\times}((l,q)):=\mathit{dom}(l)
,

•

\delta_{\times}((l,q),(l^{\prime},q^{\prime})):=\delta(l,l^{\prime})\land\bigvee_{\{a\in 2^{\mathcal{P}}\mid q^{\prime}=\delta_{M}(q,a)\}}\left(\left(\bigwedge_{\alpha\in\mathcal{P}\cap a}\alpha\right)\land\left(\bigwedge_{\alpha\in\mathcal{P}\setminus a}\lnot\alpha\right)\right)
,

and, furthermore,
\rho=(l_{0},q_{0})(l_{1},q_{1})\ldots\in\Lambda_{\times}
if and only if
\mathit{Verdict}(q_{i})\neq\mathsf{UNSAT}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, and

•

l_{0}l_{1}l_{2}\dots\in\Lambda
, or

•

there exists
i\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathit{Verdict}(q_{i})=\mathsf{SAFETY}
.

Intuitively, the product transition function ensures that the monitor’s execution is synchronized with the transitions of the game.
Note that the product game is also deterministic, as the monitor is deterministic and cannot transition to different states for the same valuation.
Furthermore, the monitor’s transition function is total w.r.t. all possible valuations of the variables, meaning that the product is non-blocking.
Hence, the resulting game structure is well-defined.
The product winning condition ensures, together with the definition of monitor, that if the monitor assigns an
\mathsf{UNSAT}
verdict, then the respective play is losing for the system.
Otherwise, in order to win, the system player has to either satisfy the original winning condition, or reach a product state where the monitor assigns a
\mathsf{SAFETY}
verdict and subsequently avoid reaching a state with an
\mathsf{UNSAT}
verdict.

The next theorem establishes that when
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
and
M
are a symbolic game and a monitor for an
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
formula
\varphi
, then the game-monitor product encodes the realizability of
\varphi
.

Theorem 5.4 (Product Correctness).

Let
\varphi\in\mathit{RP{-}LTL}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}\cup\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
be a formula,

(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
be a symbolic game for
\varphi
, with

\mathcal{G}=(L,l_{\mathit{init}},\mathbb{I},\mathbb{X},\mathit{dom},\delta)
, and let

M=(Q,q_{\mathit{init}},\mathcal{P},\delta_{M},\mathit{Verdict})
be a monitor for
\varphi
.
Let
(\mathcal{G}_{\times},\Lambda_{\times})

be the game-monitor product for
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
and
M
.

The formula
\varphi
is realizable if and only if

((l_{\mathit{init}},q_{\mathit{init}}),\mathbf{x})\in\mathit{Win}_{\mathit{Sys}}(\llbracket\mathcal{G}_{\times}\rrbracket,\llbracket\Lambda_{\times}\rrbracket)

for every assignment
\mathbf{x}\in\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X})
with
\mathbf{x}\models_{T}\mathit{dom}((l_{\mathit{init}},q_{\mathit{init}}))
.

6.
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
Monitor Construction

In this section, we describe how we construct a monitor for a given
\Phi\in\mathit{RP{-}LTL}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}\cup\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
.
Intuitively, the states of the monitor are formulas generated from
\Phi
that track the current obligations.
More concretely, each state corresponds to a formula that needs to hold on the suffix of an infinite sequence whose prefix reaches this state in order for the sequence to satisfy
\Phi
.
Clearly, if the formula associated with a state is
\bot
, we can assign verdict
\mathsf{UNSAT}
to that state.
If this is not the case, we apply logical reasoning to the state in order to simplify it, and potentially enable a simplification of the winning condition of the game or facilitate useful verdicts in successor states.

In the following subsections, we present the construction of the monitor
M=(Q,q_{\mathit{init}},\mathcal{P},\delta,\mathit{Verdict})
.

•

Q
is defined in
Section

6.1
such that each state corresponds to an
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}\cup\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
formula.
The initial state
q_{\mathit{init}}
is the state corresponding to
\Phi
.

•

The transition function is defined by
\delta(q,a):=\mathit{applyRules}(\mathit{nextState}(q,a))
.

–

In
Section

6.2
, we give
\mathit{nextState}
which computes successors using temporal expansion.

–

In
Section

6.3
, we present
\mathit{applyRules}
which applies semantic transformation rules to a state using logical reasoning.
Those rules are the key ingredient of the monitor construction as they enable a more refined verdict assignment.

•

\mathcal{P}
is defined as
\mathit{Atoms}(\Phi)\cup\mathit{PropPreds}\cup\mathit{GenPreds}
where
\mathit{PropPreds}
are predicates introduced in
Section

6.2
and
\mathit{GenPreds}
are predicates generated by a rule in
Section

6.3
.

•

\mathit{Verdict}
is defined in
Section

6.5
depending on the formula represented by each state.

6.1.
Monitor State Space

\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathit{Closure}(\alpha)&:=\{\alpha,\top,\bot\}\\
\mathit{Closure}(\lnot\varphi)&:=\mathit{Closure}(\varphi)\cup\{\lnot\psi\mid\psi\in\mathit{Closure}(\varphi)\}\\
\mathit{Closure}(\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2})&:=\mathit{Closure}(\varphi_{1})\cup\mathit{Closure}(\varphi_{2})\cup\{\psi_{1}\land\psi_{2}\mid\psi_{i}\in\mathit{Closure}(\varphi_{i}),i\in\{1,2\}\}\\

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\psi\mid\psi\in\mathit{Closure}(\varphi)\}\\
\mathit{Closure}(\varphi_{1}\LTLu\varphi_{2})&:=\mathit{Closure}(\varphi_{1})\cup\mathit{Closure}(\varphi_{2})\cup\{\psi_{1}\LTLu\psi_{2}\mid\psi_{i}\in\mathit{Closure}(\varphi_{i}),i\in\{1,2\}\}\end{array}

Figure 1.
Definition of
\mathit{Closure}:\mathit{RP{-}LTL}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}\cup\mathbb{X}^{\prime})\to 2^{\mathit{RP{-}LTL}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}\cup\mathbb{X}^{\prime})}
.

We assume w.l.o.g. that
\Phi
is an implication of the form
\Phi_{\mathsf{A}}\rightarrow\Phi_{\mathsf{G}}
, where

\Phi_{\mathsf{A}}
is a conjunction of
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
formulas representing the specification’s assumptions and

\Phi_{\mathsf{G}}
is a conjunction of
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
formulas representing the guarantees.
Note that this is a common form for specifications.

We construct the states
Q
using formulas obtained from
\Phi

by applying temporal expansion laws.
To this end, we first define in
Figure

1
the
closure
of an
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
formula, which contains its subformulas and their combinations that can result from applying the expansion laws.

Semantically, each state
q\in Q
of the constructed monitor corresponds to an
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
formula, that is, it tracks the temporal properties that have to be satisfied by the infinite sequences in the language associated with that state.
For the purpose of the monitor construction, we represent
q
as a tuple of sets of
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
formulas, that represent different parts of the formula associated with
q
.

Formally, a state
q\in Q
of the constructed monitor is a tuple

q=\langle F_{\mathsf{A}},E_{\mathsf{A}},F_{\mathsf{G}},E_{\mathsf{G}},\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}\rangle

with components which are sets of
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
formulas such that

•

F_{\mathsf{A}},E_{\mathsf{A}},F_{\mathsf{G}},E_{\mathsf{G}}\subseteq\mathit{BoolComb}(\mathit{Closure}(\Phi)\cup\mathcal{P})
, and

•

\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}\subseteq\{\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\gamma\to\varphi)\mid\varphi\in\{\alpha,\operatorname{\hbox to7.41pt{\vbox to7.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.70276pt\lower-1.11943pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {

\lxSVG@closescope }

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.19 L 0 7.74 L 8.94 7.74 L 8.94 -1.19 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\alpha\},\gamma,\alpha\in\mathit{BoolComb}(\mathcal{P})\}
.

We now explain the role of the individual components of a state
q\in Q
.

Intuitively, the sets
F_{\mathsf{A}}\cup E_{\mathsf{A}}
and
F_{\mathsf{G}}\cup E_{\mathsf{G}}
represent the current assumptions and guarantees respectively.
More precisely,
we associate with
q
the
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
formula
\mathit{Formula}(q)
defined as

\mathit{Formula}(q):=\llparenthesis F_{\mathsf{A}}\cup E_{\mathsf{A}}\rrparenthesis\rightarrow\llparenthesis F_{\mathsf{G}}\cup E_{\mathsf{G}}\rrparenthesis,

where for a set
F
of formulas, we denote by
\llparenthesis F\rrparenthesis:=\bigwedge_{\varphi\in F}\varphi
the conjunction of all elements of
F
.

The sets
\mathit{Imp}_{A}
and
\mathit{Imp}_{G}
are used to accumulate additional assumptions and guarantees that are “
implied
” by any possible path in the monitor from the initial state to
q
.

The separation into the components
F_{D}
and
E_{D}
for each
D\in\{\mathsf{A},\mathsf{G}\}
facilitates the derivation and utilization of elements in
\mathit{Imp}_{D}
, while avoiding circular reasoning. In particular, elements of
\mathit{Imp}_{D}
are deduced using the
E_{D}
component and used to simplify formulas in the
F_{D}
component.
The formulas in
\mathit{Imp}_{D}
are all of the form
\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.19 L 0 7.74 L 8.94 7.74 L 8.94 -1.19 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\psi
, meaning that
\psi
always holds from that state onwards.
Each
\psi
is an implication with a premise
\gamma
that is a non-temporal formula, which allows for checking locally if
\gamma
is implied by the current state.
We will see more concretely in
Section

6.3
, how the specific form of the states in
Q
is utilized in the construction of the monitor’s transition function.

Finiteness of
Q

By definition,
Q
is required to be finite.
The set
\mathit{Closure}(\Phi)
is finite.
To ensure that the set
\mathcal{P}
remains finite, we
ensure that the sets
\mathit{PropPreds}
and
\mathit{GenPreds}
remain finite throughout the construction of the monitor,
as explained in
Section

6.2
and
Section

6.3
, respectively.
While, there are infinitely many syntactically different Boolean combinations over
\mathit{Closure}(\Phi)\cup\mathcal{P}
, there are only finitely many of them up to propositional equivalence.
For forming the sets
F_{\mathsf{A}},E_{\mathsf{A}},F_{\mathsf{G}}
, and
E_{\mathsf{G}}
we use unique representatives of the respective equivalence classes, and hence, there are only finitely many instances of
F_{\mathsf{A}},E_{\mathsf{A}},F_{\mathsf{G}}
, and
E_{\mathsf{G}}
.
Similarly for
\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}}
and
\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}
.

We remark that the state space of the monitor is constructed on-the-fly, without computing
\mathit{Closure}(\Phi)
upfront and many elements of
\mathit{Closure}(\Phi)
are usually not reachable in the monitor.

Initial State

The formula associated with the monitor’s initial state
q_{\mathit{init}}
is
\Phi
.
We define

q_{\mathit{init}}:=\langle F^{0}_{\mathsf{A}},E^{0}_{\mathsf{A}},F^{0}_{\mathsf{G}},E^{0}_{\mathsf{G}},\emptyset,\emptyset\rangle

where for
D\in\{\mathsf{A},\mathsf{G}\}
, the sets
F^{0}_{D}
and
E^{0}_{D}
partition the set of conjuncts of
\Phi_{D}
.
We discuss the concrete choice of elements of
F_{D}
and
E_{D}
in
Section

6.3
.

.

Correctness Property

In the rest of this section, we present the construction of the transition function
\delta
and the verdict-labelling function
\mathit{Verdict}
, after which we will show that the constructed monitor satisfies the conditions in
Definition

5.2
.
To this end, we will show that

for every
q\in Q
,
\nu\in\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I})
and

\pi\cdot\nu\cdot\rho\in\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I})^{\omega}
with

\delta^{*}_{M}(\pi\cdot\nu)=q
:

(1)

\pi\cdot\nu\cdot\rho\models\Phi\text{ if and only if }\nu\cdot\rho\models\mathit{Formula}(q).

We refer to (
1
) as the
monitor-state correctness property
.

6.2.
Next-State Construction Using Expansion for
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
Formulas

The states of the monitor correspond to temporal formulas that track properties that have to hold for an infinite sequence from this step on.
In order to compute the successors of such a state, we split the formula into a part that has to hold immediately in the current step, and a part that has to hold later in the future.
We do so by applying the expansion laws of the temporal operators
(
Baier and Katoen, 2008
)
.

To construct
\mathit{nextState}(q,a)
for a state
q
and a letter
a
, we will apply expansion to each of the formulas in the sets in
q
. Thus, we first define an expansion function for individual formulas, similar to
(
Esparza et al., 2020
;
Vardi, 1994
)
, and then we explain how we use this function to compute the successors of states in
Q
.

Expansion Function for
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
Formulas

Before we present the formal definition of the expansion operation, consider as a simple example the formula
\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.19 L 0 7.74 L 8.94 7.74 L 8.94 -1.19 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(i>0\to\operatorname{\hbox to7.41pt{\vbox to7.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.70276pt\lower-1.11943pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 M 4.77 3.57 C 4.77 6.21 2.63 8.34 0 8.34 C -2.63 8.34 -4.77 6.21 -4.77 3.57 C -4.77 0.94 -2.63 -1.19 0 -1.19 C 2.63 -1.19 4.77 0.94 4.77 3.57 Z M 0 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(x^{\prime}>x))
.
This formula, by the expansion laws, is equivalent to
(i>0\to\operatorname{\hbox to7.41pt{\vbox to7.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.70276pt\lower-1.11943pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {

, which splits the formula into a “current part” and the part under a next operator.

.
This resulting formula has only requirements that have to be checked in the future.

, that is, the same obligation and the variable
x
has to become larger in the following step.
Analogously, if

holds in the current letter, then the successor formula is
\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {

.

We now proceed with the formal definition.
Let us denote with
\mathit{BC}(\Phi):=\mathit{BoolComb}(\mathit{Closure}(\Phi)\cup\mathcal{P})
the set of Boolean combinations of the elements of
\mathit{Closure}(\Phi)\cup\mathcal{P}
.
We define the expansion operation

\mathit{expand}:\mathit{BC}(\Phi)\times 2^{\mathcal{P}}\to\mathit{BC}(\Phi)

to take a formula as used in
Q
and a subset of predicates of
\mathcal{P}
and return the successor element. The
\mathit{expand}
operation folds the application of the expansion laws, the assignment of the predicates in
\mathcal{P}
, and the removal of the
\operatorname{\hbox to7.41pt{\vbox to7.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.70276pt\lower-1.11943pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 M 4.77 3.57 C 4.77 6.21 2.63 8.34 0 8.34 C -2.63 8.34 -4.77 6.21 -4.77 3.57 C -4.77 0.94 -2.63 -1.19 0 -1.19 C 2.63 -1.19 4.77 0.94 4.77 3.57 Z M 0 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
operator into one:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathit{expand}(\alpha,a)&:=\top&\text{ if }\alpha\in a\\
\mathit{expand}(\alpha,a)&:=\bot&\text{ if }\alpha\not\in a\\
\mathit{expand}(\lnot\varphi,a)&:=\lnot\mathit{expand}(\varphi,a)\\
\mathit{expand}(\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2},a)&:=\mathit{expand}(\varphi_{1},a)\land\mathit{expand}(\varphi_{2},a)\\
\mathit{expand}(\operatorname{\hbox to7.41pt{\vbox to7.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.70276pt\lower-1.11943pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 M 4.77 3.57 C 4.77 6.21 2.63 8.34 0 8.34 C -2.63 8.34 -4.77 6.21 -4.77 3.57 C -4.77 0.94 -2.63 -1.19 0 -1.19 C 2.63 -1.19 4.77 0.94 4.77 3.57 Z M 0 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\varphi,a)&:=\varphi\\
\mathit{expand}(\varphi_{1}\LTLu\varphi_{2},a)&:=\mathit{expand}(\varphi_{2},a)\lor\mathit{expand}(\varphi_{1},a)\land(\varphi_{1}\LTLu\varphi_{2}).\\
\end{array}

Note that
\mathit{expand}
is well-typed, as it only generates elements of
\mathit{BC}(\Phi)
.

The function
\mathit{expand}
has the following property, which is crucial for the correctness of the monitor: An infinite execution
\rho
that is consistent with an assignment
a
to the predicates, satisfies a formula
\varphi
if and only if the suffix
\rho[1,\infty)
satisfies
\mathit{expand}(\varphi,a)
. This is formally stated below.

Lemma 6.2.

For all
\rho\in{(\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}))}^{\omega}
and
a\subseteq\mathcal{P}
where

\langle\rho[0],\rho[1]\rangle\models_{T}\left(\bigwedge_{\alpha\in\mathcal{P}\cap a}\alpha\right)\land\left(\bigwedge_{\alpha\in\mathcal{P}\setminus a}\lnot\alpha\right)
,
it holds that

\rho\models\varphi
if and only if
\rho_{+1}\models\mathit{expand}(\varphi,a)
.

Remark on computing
\mathit{expand}
:

To compute the expansion we do not enumerate
2^{\mathcal{P}}
but branch on occurrences inside the formula, as often not all predicates are relevant to the current step.
Also, we remove selections
a\in 2^{\mathcal{P}}
that are inconsistent on the theory level, i.e. if the conjuction of predicates in
a
is unsatisifable, as those selection will never be enabled anyways.
This is a simple, local application of theory-level reasoning.
For example, in
x=0\lor x>0\land\operatorname{\hbox to7.41pt{\vbox to7.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.70276pt\lower-1.11943pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 M 4.77 3.57 C 4.77 6.21 2.63 8.34 0 8.34 C -2.63 8.34 -4.77 6.21 -4.77 3.57 C -4.77 0.94 -2.63 -1.19 0 -1.19 C 2.63 -1.19 4.77 0.94 4.77 3.57 Z M 0 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}y=0
, the predicate
y=0
is irrelevant for the current step and the selection
\{x=0,x>0,\dots\}
is inconsistent.

Predicate Propagation

The application of
\mathit{expand}(\varphi,a)
checks the “current” part of the formula
\varphi
against the letter
a
.
However, as the values of the variables
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
are related to the values of
\mathbb{X}
at the next step,
\varphi
and
a
might imply some additional properties that are satisfied at the next step, but which are not propagated into
\mathit{expand}(\varphi,a)
.
For example, consider the formula
\varphi:=x=0\land x^{\prime}=x\land\operatorname{\hbox to7.41pt{\vbox to7.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.70276pt\lower-1.11943pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 M 4.77 3.57 C 4.77 6.21 2.63 8.34 0 8.34 C -2.63 8.34 -4.77 6.21 -4.77 3.57 C -4.77 0.94 -2.63 -1.19 0 -1.19 C 2.63 -1.19 4.77 0.94 4.77 3.57 Z M 0 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(x=1)
, which is unsatisfiable.
However, for
a=\{x=0,x^{\prime}=x,\dots\}
the expansion will yield
x=1
which is satisfiable.
While this is irrelevant for the correctness of the monitor,
it is sometimes useful to
propagate these additional properties to the next step, and strengthen
\mathit{expand}(\varphi,a)
.
For instance, in the above example, we would like to propagate the information that
x=0
in the next step.

To this end, we fix at the start of the monitor construction a set
\mathit{PropPreds}\subseteq\mathit{QF}(\mathbb{X})
of formulas whose value we want to propagate. This set depends on
\Phi
. One suitable choice is the set of elements of
\mathit{Atoms}(\Phi)
that belong to
\mathit{QF}(\mathbb{X})
plus their negations.
We discuss this further in
Section

7
.

For
a\in 2^{\mathcal{P}}
, we can compute the set of formulas in
\mathit{PropPreds}
that hold in the next step as

\mathit{propagate}(a):=\left\{\beta\in\mathit{PropPreds}\mid\forall\mathbb{X},\mathbb{I},\mathbb{X}^{\prime}.~\left(\bigwedge_{\alpha\in\mathcal{P}\cap a}\alpha\right)\land\left(\bigwedge_{\alpha\in\mathcal{P}\setminus a}\lnot\alpha\right)\to\beta[\mathbb{X}\mapsto\mathbb{X}^{\prime}]\text{ is valid}\right\}.

Intuitively, we just check which elements of
\mathit{PropPreds}
always hold in the next step after our current assignment
a\in 2^{\mathcal{P}}
.
In our example from above, this would be the case for
x=0
, since
x=0\land x^{\prime}=x\to x^{\prime}=0
is indeed valid, and we can propagate
x=0
to the successor.

Successor-State Computation

We have shown how to apply expansion to a single
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
formula, and how to compute additional predicates that we can propagate.
We now put those together, and define the function
\mathit{nextState}
.
Intuitively, we apply
\mathit{expand}
individually to all formulas in

E_{\mathsf{A}}
,
F_{\mathsf{A}}
,
E_{\mathsf{G}}
, and
F_{\mathsf{G}}
.
We add the propagated elements of
\mathit{PropPreds}
to
E_{\mathsf{G}}
, which maintains our form.
We do not apply
\mathit{expand}
to
\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}}
and
\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}
, since they are not part of
\mathit{Formula}(q)
, the formula associated with a state
q
.
In fact, as we will see in
Section

6.3
, the elements of
\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}}
and
\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}
are derived from the other components of
q
and the paths in the monitor leading to
q
.

Formally, for a monitor state
q=\langle F_{\mathsf{A}},E_{\mathsf{A}},F_{\mathsf{G}},E_{\mathsf{G}},\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}\rangle
and
a\in 2^{\mathcal{P}}
we define

\mathit{nextState}(q,a):=\langle\mathit{exps}(F_{\mathsf{A}}),\mathit{exps}(E_{\mathsf{A}}),\mathit{exps}(F_{\mathsf{G}}),\mathit{exps}(E_{\mathsf{G}})\cup\mathit{propagate}(a),\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}\rangle

where
\mathit{exps}(F):=\{\mathit{expand}(\varphi,a)\mid\varphi\in F\}
for
F\subseteq\mathit{RP{-}LTL}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}\cup\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
.

6.3.
Rules for Transformation of Monitor States

In principle, using the function
\mathit{expand}
defined in
Section

6.2
, we can construct a monitor for
\Phi
.
Such a monitor, however, will be of little use, since the verdicts that it can assign will be based purely on the temporal expansion laws and the aforementioned pruned predicate selections.
The goal of our monitor construction, however, is to perform a combination of first-order and temporal logic reasoning that will enable the desired game simplifications envisioned in
Section

2
.

To achieve this, we define a function
\mathit{applyRules}
which performs a sequence of transformations on state
\mathit{expand}(q,a)
to compute the successor monitor state
\delta(q,a)
.
These transformations modify the state by applying local theory reasoning, high-level reasoning over the temporal operators, and most importantly reasoning that connects the theory reasoning applied over time with the temporal aspect of the state.
The ultimate aim of these transformations is to transform, when possible a state
q_{1}
into a state
q_{2}
, where

\mathit{Formula}(q_{2})
is
\bot
or a syntactic safety formula.
If this is the case, then the monitor can assign the verdict
\mathsf{UNSAT}
or
\mathsf{SAFETY}
respectively, thus enabling pruning of the game or simplification of the winning condition when building the product of the monitor with the game.
For states that cannot be reformulated to
\bot
or syntactic safety formula, the transformation might allow better pruning of the predicate selection between states or enable useful reasoning in a successor state by simplification or deriving new information.

We will now define a set of allowed transformations,
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
-
\mathit{Rules}
, each of which is in the form of a rule whose
premises
characterize the monitor states to which this rule is applicable, and an
effect
that describes the state modification.
The transformation rules will make heavy use of the sets
\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}}
and
\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}
, which did not play a role in the definition of
\mathit{expand}
which left them unmodified.

As stated earlier,
\mathit{Imp}_{A}
and
\mathit{Imp}_{G}
are used to accumulate additional assumptions and guarantees that are “implied” by any possible path in the monitor from the initial state to a given state
q
.
More formally, the rules in
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
-
\mathit{Rules}
, which will extend and use the sets
\mathit{Imp}_{D}
, will be such that the following
implied-state correctness property
is satisfied:
for every
q=\langle F_{\mathsf{A}},E_{\mathsf{A}},F_{\mathsf{G}},E_{\mathsf{G}},\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}\rangle
, and every

\nu\in\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I})
and

\pi\cdot\nu\cdot\rho\in\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I})^{\omega}
with

\delta^{*}_{M}(\pi\cdot\nu)=q
:

(2)

\begin{array}[]{lll}\text{if }\nu\cdot\rho\models\llparenthesis E_{\mathsf{A}}\rrparenthesis&\text{ then }&\nu\cdot\rho\models\llparenthesis\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}}\rrparenthesis\\
\text{if }\nu\cdot\rho\models\llparenthesis E_{\mathsf{A}}\cup E_{\mathsf{G}}\rrparenthesis&\text{ then }&\nu\cdot\rho\models\llparenthesis\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}\rrparenthesis.\end{array}

Intuitively, this property states that the properties satisfied by any prefix reaching
q
, together with the current assumptions in the set
E_{\mathsf{A}}
(respectively assumptions plus guarantees in
E_{\mathsf{A}}\cup E_{\mathsf{G}}
) imply the formulas collected in the set
\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}}
(respectively the set
\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}
).

Transformation Soundness.

In order to guarantee that the resulting monitor satisfies the monitor-sate correctness property (
1
),
we will ensure that its states satisfy the implied-state correctness property (
2
).
To facilitate this, we provide a local criterion, termed
sound monitor-state transformation
, for the state transformation rules that guarantees the global property (
2
).

Definition 6.3 (Sound Monitor-State Transformation).

A
sound monitor-state transformation
is a partial function
\textsc{Transform}:Q\to Q
such that for every

q_{1}=\langle F_{A}^{1},E_{\mathsf{A}}^{1},F_{\mathsf{G}}^{1},E_{\mathsf{G}}^{1},\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}}^{1},\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}^{1}\rangle
and

q_{2}=\langle F_{\mathsf{A}}^{2},E_{\mathsf{A}}^{2},F_{\mathsf{G}}^{2},E_{\mathsf{G}}^{2},\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}}^{2},\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}^{2}\rangle

with
\textsc{Transform}(q_{1})=q_{2}
the following conditions are satisfied:

(a)

\llparenthesis F_{\mathsf{A}}^{1}\cup E_{\mathsf{A}}^{1}\cup\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}}^{1}\rrparenthesis\rightarrow\llparenthesis F_{\mathsf{G}}^{1}\cup E_{\mathsf{G}}^{1}\cup\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}^{1}\rrparenthesis\equiv\llparenthesis F_{\mathsf{A}}^{2}\cup E_{\mathsf{A}}^{2}\cup\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}}^{2}\rrparenthesis\rightarrow\llparenthesis F_{\mathsf{G}}^{2}\cup E_{\mathsf{G}}^{2}\cup\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}^{2}\rrparenthesis

(b)

the following two implications are valid

\begin{array}[]{lll}\llparenthesis E_{\mathsf{A}}^{2}\cup\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}}^{1}\rrparenthesis&\rightarrow&\llparenthesis\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}}^{2}\rrparenthesis,\\
\llparenthesis E_{\mathsf{A}}^{2}\cup E_{\mathsf{G}}^{2}\cup\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}^{1}\cup\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}}^{1}\rrparenthesis&\rightarrow&\llparenthesis\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}^{2}\rrparenthesis.\end{array}

(c)

the implications

\llparenthesis E_{D}^{2}\rrparenthesis\rightarrow\llparenthesis E_{D}^{1}\rrparenthesis
for
D\in\{\mathsf{A},\mathsf{G}\}
are valid.

Condition (a) requires equivalence on the formula level, including the implied formulas, while condition (b) is the localized version of the global property (
2
). Condition (c) states that
E_{D}
can only be strengthened.
Any rule that defines a transformation satisfying the conditions in
Definition

6.3
can be added to our set of rules
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
-
\mathit{Rules}
while maintaining correctness of the monitor.

We define several operations and useful sets of formulas that are used in the rules’ formalization.

Substitution in Rules.

The effect of a rule is often described by means of substitution.
For a set
F\subseteq\mathit{RP{-}LTL}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}\cup\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
of formulas and formulas
\varphi,\psi\in\mathit{RP{-}LTL}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}\cup\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
we denote with

F[\varphi\mapsto\psi]
the set of formulas obtained from
F
by simultaneously substituting in each of them each occurrence of
\varphi
by
\psi
.
Furthermore, we denote by

F[\varphi\mapsto_{non-nested}\psi]
the set of formulas obtained from
F
by simultaneously substituting in each of them each
non-nested
occurrence of
\varphi
by
\psi
.
Here, a
non-nested
occurrence of
\varphi
in a formula
\theta
is one that is not in the scope of a temporal operator.

Useful State Components.

For the premises of rules, two types of formulas extracted from a state’s components are of particular interest.
These are the non-temporal assumptions and guarantees that must be satisfied in the
current
step, and the state properties that must hold for all steps in the future.
We refer to the latter as the
implied invariants
associated with the given state.
Formally, for a state
q=\langle F_{\mathsf{A}},E_{\mathsf{A}},F_{\mathsf{G}},E_{\mathsf{G}},\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}},\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}\rangle
, we define the following formulas in
\mathit{QF}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}\cup\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathit{Curr}_{\mathsf{A}}(q)&:=&\llparenthesis\{\alpha\in\mathit{QF}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}\cup\mathbb{X}^{\prime})\mid\alpha\in E_{\mathsf{A}}\}\rrparenthesis,\\
\mathit{Curr}_{\mathsf{G}}(q)&:=&\llparenthesis\{\alpha\in\mathit{QF}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}\cup\mathbb{X}^{\prime})\mid\alpha\in E_{\mathsf{G}}\cup E_{\mathsf{A}}\}\rrparenthesis\end{array}

representing the current-state assumptions and guarantees, and

\mathit{ImpInv}_{D}(q):=\llparenthesis\{\alpha\in\mathit{QF}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}\cup\mathbb{X}^{\prime})\mid\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.19 L 0 7.74 L 8.94 7.74 L 8.94 -1.19 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\alpha\in\mathit{Imp}_{D}\}\rrparenthesis\quad\quad\text{ for }D\in\{\mathsf{A},\mathsf{G}\}

representing the implied invariants.
Note that we add the current assumptions
\mathit{Curr}_{\mathsf{A}}(q)
as part of the current guarantees, as the guarantees must be satisfied only if the assumptions are.

Example 6.4.

Reconsider the state
q
from
Example

6.1
where additionally

\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}}=\{\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {

and

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 M 4.77 3.57 C 4.77 6.21 2.63 8.34 0 8.34 C -2.63 8.34 -4.77 6.21 -4.77 3.57 C -4.77 0.94 -2.63 -1.19 0 -1.19 C 2.63 -1.19 4.77 0.94 4.77 3.57 Z M 0 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

.
We have that

\mathit{Curr}_{\mathsf{A}}(q)=(i=0)
,

\mathit{Curr}_{\mathsf{G}}(q)=(i=0\land x=0)
,

\mathit{ImpInv}_{\mathsf{A}}(q)=i>0
, and

\mathit{ImpInv}_{\mathsf{G}}(q)=x^{\prime}-y=x
.

We are now ready to present the state transformation rules of our monitor construction.
We group the rules in two categories:
formula simplification rules
and
rules for deriving implied formulas
.
Intuitively, the formula simplification rules simplify the formulas represented by the sets
F_{D}
and
E_{D}
for
D\in\{\mathsf{A},\mathsf{G}\}
, thus possibly bringing the state closer to one where the associated formula is such that a verdict can be assigned.
The rules for deriving implied formulas extend the sets
\mathit{Imp}_{D}
possibly enabling further applications of the simplification rules.

6.3.1.
Simplification Rules

The formula simplification rules, shown in
Figure

2
, serve two purposes.
First, rewriting the formulas in a state
q
into simpler ones, or into forms that facilitate the application of other rules.
Second, replacing formulas or sub-formulas in
q
with constants (i.e., with
\top
or
\bot
). We propose three types of simplification rules: rules for
formula rewriting
,
rules for
detecting unsatisfiability
of
\llparenthesis F_{\mathsf{A}}\cup E_{\mathsf{A}}\cup\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}}\rrparenthesis
or
\llparenthesis F_{\mathsf{G}}\cup E_{\mathsf{G}}\cup\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}\rrparenthesis
and rules for
sub-formula substitution
.

Formula Rewriting.

As
formula rewriting
rules we consider state transformations based on known equivalences between
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
formulas (in fact, between LTL formulas). As one example, consider the replacement of every occurrence of
\varphi\LTLw\bot
in
q
by
\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.465pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.07 L 0 6.97 L 8.04 6.97 L 8.04 -1.07 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\gamma\rightarrow\varphi)\in\mathit{Imp}_{D}

\mathit{Curr}_{D}(q)\land\mathit{ImpInv}_{D}(q)\models_{T}\gamma

(Simplify-Non-Nested)

F_{D}^{\prime}:=F_{D}[\varphi\mapsto_{non-nested}\top]

Figure 2.
Formula simplification rules. Each rule is given by its set of premisses and its effect on the monitor state, where
D\in\{\mathsf{A},\mathsf{G}\}
. Components of the state not assigned in the effect are not modified. by the rule.

Detecting Unsatisfiability.

We propose several rules for detecting logical inconsistency, which play a crucial role in identifying states
q
where the assumptions or the guarantees are equivalent to
\bot
.

Rule
(UNSAT)
is applicable when for some
D\in\{\mathsf{A},\mathsf{G}\}
the formula
\mathit{Curr}_{D}(q)
, which should be satisfied at the current time-point and the formula
\mathit{ImpInv}_{D}(q)
which should be satisfied for all future time-points, including the current, contradict each other. In such case, we replace the assumption or guarantee (depending on
D
) component of
q
by
\bot
. As a result, a subsequent application of the formula rewriting transformation rules will reduce the formula associated with
q
to a constant.

Rule
(UNSAT-
\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
)
detects contradicting liveness and invariants assumptions or guarantees. It is applicable when there is an element of some
\mathit{Imp}_{D}
of the form

\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\gamma\rightarrow\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {

is must hold in the current time-point and

, which in this case must be satisfied eventually, contradicts the invariants in
\mathit{Imp}_{D}
.

is unsatisfiable.

and

)
can be checked using an SMT solver.

Sub-Formula Substitution.

The rules above are applicable when the overall assumption or guarantee component of a state is unsatisfiable.
The rest of the rules shown in
Figure

2
, on the other hand, are applicable to individual sub-formulas, and allow for substituting them by the constants
\top
or
\bot
, or by some of their sub-formulas.
Overall, this leads to simplification of the state.

To avoid circular reasoning, the substitution operations are applied only to the
F_{\mathsf{A}}
and
F_{\mathsf{G}}
components of
q
, which are not used for deriving the
\mathit{Curr}_{D}(q)
and
\mathit{ImpInv}_{D}(q)
formulas.

Rules
(Subst-
\top
)
and
(Subst-
\bot
)
consider formulas
\gamma\in\mathit{QF}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}\cup\mathbb{X}^{\prime})

that appear as sub-formulas in some
F_{D}
.
If
\gamma
(or its negation) is entailed by the implied invariants
\mathit{ImpInv}_{D}(q)
,
we can replace
\gamma
by
\top
(or
\bot
, respectively) in
F_{D}
,
since the elements of
F_{D}
are not used in the derivation of
\mathit{Imp}_{D}(q)
.

Rules
(Simplify-
\rightarrow
)
and
(Simplify-
\land
)
directly match elements of the set
\mathit{Imp}_{D}
for some
D\in\{\mathsf{A},\mathsf{G}\}
with sub-formulas in
F_{D}
.
Using the fact that
\gamma\rightarrow\varphi
must hold at every point in time,
we can apply the respective substitutions to all occurrences of
\gamma\rightarrow\varphi
(respectively
\gamma\land\varphi
) in
F_{D}
.

Rule
(Simplify-Non-Nested)
.

The previous sub-formula substitution rules replace formulas entailed by, or elements of,
\mathit{Imp}_{D}
.
Since each formula
\gamma\rightarrow\varphi
with
\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.19 L 0 7.74 L 8.94 7.74 L 8.94 -1.19 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\gamma\rightarrow\varphi)\in\mathit{Imp}_{D}
must be satisfied at all points in time, these rules can apply replacement of any occurrences of the respective formulas in
F_{D}
.
The last simplification rule,
(Simplify-Non-Nested)
, on the other hand, considers elements

\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.19 L 0 7.74 L 8.94 7.74 L 8.94 -1.19 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\gamma\rightarrow\varphi)
of
\mathit{Imp}_{D}
where
the formula
\gamma
is entailed by the conjunction of the implied invariants and the formula
\mathit{Curr}_{D}(q)
.
Since
\mathit{Curr}_{D}(q)
is only required in the current time-point, the rule
(Simplify-Non-Nested)
only replaces occurrences of
\varphi
pertaining to the current point in time, that is, those not in the scope of a temporal operator.
