.

6.3.2.
Rules for Deducing Formulas in
\mathit{Imp}_{D}

The transformation rules presented so far do not modify the sets
\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}}
and
\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}
, but rely on their elements for
the satisfaction of their premises.
Our second category of rules derives implied formulas and adds them to
\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}}
and
\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}
.
We distinguish three types of such rules.
First, we have rules that
propagate formulas to
\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}}
and
\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}
from the sets
E_{\mathsf{A}}
and
E_{\mathsf{G}}
respectively.
The second type of rules are of crucial importance, as they
generate invariants and reachability properties
that are
guaranteed to hold for the infinite sequences in the language of the respective state.
These rules integrate temporal and first-order logical reasoning to derive consequences which can enable the application of the rules in the first category.
Finally, we have a set of rules that allow us to
combine different elements of each of the sets
\mathit{Imp}_{D}
for
D\in\{\mathsf{A},\mathsf{G}\}
.

All the formulas that the rules add to the set
\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}}
are implied by formulas in
E_{\mathsf{A}}\cup\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}}
,
and all the formulas added to
\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}
are implied by
E_{\mathsf{A}}\cup E_{\mathsf{G}}\cup\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}\cup\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}}
.
Furthermore, they do not modify the sets
E_{\mathsf{A}}
and
E_{\mathsf{G}}
.
Thus, they satisfy condition (b) in
Definition

6.3
.
The rules modify
\mathit{Imp}_{D}
by adding new elements to them, and leave the sets
F_{D}
and
E_{D}
unchanged. Therefore, the resulting transformations satisfy conditions (a) and (c) in
Definition

6.3
.

Propagating Formulas to
\mathit{Imp}_{D}
.

The rules
(Propagate-
\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.19 L 0 7.74 L 8.94 7.74 L 8.94 -1.19 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
)
and
(Propagate-
\LTLw
)
shown in
Figure

4
add to the set
\mathit{Imp}_{D}
formulas of the appropriate form that are present in the set
E_{D}
or entailed by formulas in
E_{D}
.
The rule
(Propagate-Assump)
ensures that the formulas implied by the assumptions, i.e., those in
\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}}
are also present in the set
\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}
.

Rule
(Propagate-
\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.19 L 0 7.74 L 8.94 7.74 L 8.94 -1.19 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
)
directly matches formulas in
E_{D}
(modulo simple equivalence rewriting).
Rule
(Propagate-
\LTLw
)
identifies pairs of formulas
\alpha_{1}\LTLw\beta_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}\LTLw\beta_{2}
where the “end-condition” formulas
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
cannot be satisfied simultaneously and neither
\beta_{1}
can be satisfied while
\alpha_{2}
is true, nor the other way around.
In this case, the two formulas together entail that
\alpha_{1}\land\alpha_{2}
should hold always meaning that we can add
\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.19 L 0 7.74 L 8.94 7.74 L 8.94 -1.19 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\top\rightarrow\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.19 L 0 7.74 L 8.94 7.74 L 8.94 -1.19 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\alpha_{1}\land\alpha_{2}))
to
\mathit{Imp}_{D}
.

Combination of Implied Formulas.

The transformation rules shown in
Figure

3
enable the extension of the sets
\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}}
and
\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}
by combining existing elements of the respective set.
Rules
(Join-Imp)
and
(Chain-Imp)
allow for weakening the left-hand side of the implications by combining elements of
\mathit{Imp}_{D}
.

\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {

,
and new reachability properties, i.e., formulas of the form

\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.19 L 0 7.74 L 8.94 7.74 L 8.94 -1.19 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\gamma\rightarrow\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\beta)
.
The generated formulas are entailed by the current
\mathit{Imp}_{D}
and
E_{D}
sets,
and are added to
\mathit{Imp}_{D}
.
The ability to generate new implied invariants and reachability properties is crucial for “discharging” temporal obligations or detecting “temporal conflicts”.

Rule
(Gen-Inv)
, shown in
Figure

4
, establishes that whenever
\gamma
is satisfied, then all sequences of valuations that satisfy the invariants in
\mathit{ImpInv}_{D}(q)
must also satisfy
\alpha
at every point in time.
This justifies the addition of the formula
\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.19 L 0 7.74 L 8.94 7.74 L 8.94 -1.19 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\gamma\rightarrow\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.19 L 0 7.74 L 8.94 7.74 L 8.94 -1.19 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\alpha)
to
\mathit{Imp}_{D}
.
For example, if
\gamma:=(x=0)
and
\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.19 L 0 7.74 L 8.94 7.74 L 8.94 -1.19 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(x^{\prime}\geq x)
holds,
(Gen-Inv)
can prove that
\alpha:=(x\geq 0)
always holds, i.e.
\alpha
is an invariant.
The formula
\theta
is a strengthening of
\alpha
, which plays the role of an inductive invariant.
To apply this rule we need to supply the formulas
\alpha
and
\gamma
and check the respective entailments.
A suitable choice is taking
\gamma:=\mathit{Curr}_{D}(q)
and
\alpha:=\neg\beta
for some
\beta
where
\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\beta
or
\alpha_{1}\LTLw\beta
appears in
F_{D}
, which would subsequently enable the detection of liveness sub-formulas that cannot be satisfied.
As for
\theta
, we can either take
\theta:=\alpha
, or if this does not succeed, check for existence of
\theta
using for instance a solver for Constrained Horn Clauses (CHC).

)
also
\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.19 L 0 7.74 L 8.94 7.74 L 8.94 -1.19 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\top\to\lnot(x=-5))
.
This allows
(Subst-
\bot
)
to replace
x=-5
in
F_{\mathsf{G}}
by
\bot
such that
F_{\mathsf{G}}:=\{y\neq 0\}
.
Now
\mathit{Curr}_{\mathsf{G}}=y\neq 0\land x=0
and
\mathit{ImpInv}_{\mathsf{G}}
contains
x=y
.
Hence, by
(UNSAT)
we get the state
\langle\emptyset,\emptyset,\{\bot\},\{\bot\},\emptyset,\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}\rangle
.

Rule
(Gen-Inv-P)
.

The rule
(Gen-Inv)
requires the candidate invariant
\alpha
.
This is useful for identifying liveness sub-formulas present in
q
that cannot be satisfied.
However, we would also like to be able to derive the
most precise
invariant, which might be useful to simplify successors of the state
q
.
This is the goal of
(Gen-Inv-P)
in
Figure

4
which computes
\alpha
as a least fixpoint.
This fixpoint is the smallest set of assignments to the program variables
\mathbb{X}
consisting of the assignments where
\gamma
holds (the first disjunct in the fixpoint), and any possible successor assignments restricted by
\mathit{ImpInv}_{D}(q)
(the second disjunct).
Note that in the fixpoint we reformulate
\mathit{ImpInv}_{D}(q)
as a “predecessor-assignment current-assignment relation” via variable renaming.
Intuitively,
\alpha
characterizes the set of assignments that are reachable when the implied invariants hold.
A suitable application of
(Gen-Inv-P)
is with
\gamma:=\mathit{Curr}_{D}(q)
, which subsequently allows us to conclude that
\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.19 L 0 7.74 L 8.94 7.74 L 8.94 -1.19 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\alpha
must be satisfied and add it to
\mathit{Imp}_{D}
using the rule
(Chain-Imp-
\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.19 L 0 7.74 L 8.94 7.74 L 8.94 -1.19 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
)
for expanding
\mathit{Imp}_{D}
.

(Gen-Inv-P)
might generate new predicates that are not in the set
\mathcal{P}
yet.
We add those to
\mathit{GenPreds}
but need to take care to keep
\mathit{GenPreds}
finite.
Hence, we limit how many times we apply
(Gen-Inv-P)
to the same
E_{D}
. Since the number of possible
E_{D}
sets is finite and independent of
\mathit{GenPreds}
, this ensures that the set
\mathit{GenPreds}
remains finite.

)
to
\langle\emptyset,\emptyset,\{\bot\},\{\bot\},\emptyset,\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}\rangle
.
This reasoning is not possible with our remaining rules without
(Gen-Inv-P)
.

Rule
(Gen-Reach)
generates implied reachability properties.
Those are useful not only for detecting invariants that must be violated, but also for “discharging”
\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\beta
sub-formulas that are implied by other components.
The rule requires us to provide a formula
\beta
and then computes as a least fixpoint a formula
\gamma
representing all the valuations from which all sequences that satisfy the invariants in
\mathit{ImpInv}_{D}(q)
eventually satisfy
\beta
.
This justifies the addition of
\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.19 L 0 7.74 L 8.94 7.74 L 8.94 -1.19 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\gamma\rightarrow\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\beta)
to
\mathit{Imp}_{D}
.
A suitable application is with formulas
\beta
such that
\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {

\lxSVG@closescope }

or
\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\beta
appears in
F_{D}
, which enables subsequently the detection of a contradiction or that the formula is satisfied, respectively.

Example 6.8.

where

adds

.
Hence,
(Simplify-Non-Nested)
replaces
\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}x>1000
in
F_{\mathsf{G}}
to
\top

which results in the state
\langle\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\{x=0,\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {

.

6.3.3.
Application of State Transformation Rules

In the following, we discuss how and in which order we apply the transformations in
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
-
\mathit{Rules}
, in order to compute the function
\mathit{applyRules}
.

To establish the correctness of function
\mathit{applyRules}
, we prove the following statement.

Theorem 6.9.

Each rule in
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
-
\mathit{Rules}
defines a sound monitor-state transformation.

Partitioning into
F_{D}
and
E_{D}

We first discuss what parts of the assumptions and the guarantees we put in the sets
F_{D}
and
E_{D}
for
D=\mathsf{A}
and
D=\mathsf{G}
, respectively.
Since we use
E_{D}
to derive the elements of
\mathit{Imp}_{D}
,
and use the elements of
\mathit{Imp}_{D}
to perform substitutions in
F_{D}
, we are only allowed to move formulas from
F_{D}
to
E_{D}
, and
not vice versa
, in order to avoid circular reasoning.

As the rules utilize from
E_{D}
only the current obligations in
\mathit{Curr}_{D}(q)
and formulas with top-level
\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.19 L 0 7.74 L 8.94 7.74 L 8.94 -1.19 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
and
\LTLw
operators with relatively simple arguments, we place only such formulas in
E_{D}
.
Note that in that way formulas of the form
\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\varphi
remain in
F_{D}
, and hence, can potentially be “discharged”.

Rule Application Order

We apply
(Propagate-Assump)
and
(Propagate-
\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.19 L 0 7.74 L 8.94 7.74 L 8.94 -1.19 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
)
always when we modify
E_{D}
and
\mathit{Imp}_{D}
.
Furthermore, we apply formula rewriting to simplify formulas on the logical level whenever possible.
As for the other rules, we apply the in the following order:

1.

We first apply
(UNSAT)
and
(UNSAT-
\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
)
to check early if the formula is contradictory.

2.

As expansion and rule
(Propagate-
\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.19 L 0 7.74 L 8.94 7.74 L 8.94 -1.19 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
)
might enlarge
\mathit{Imp}_{D}
, we apply the chain rules and
(Propagate-
\LTLw
)
to saturate
\mathit{Imp}_{D}
with existing information to ease the next steps.

3.

Next, we use the simplification rules
(Subst-
\top
)
,
(Subst-
\bot
)
,
(Simplify-
\rightarrow
)
,
(Simplify-
\land
)
and
(Simplify-Non-Nested)
to simplify
F_{D}
utilizing the saturated set
\mathit{Imp}_{D}
.

4.

After that, we apply the generation rules
(Gen-Inv)
,
(Gen-Reach)
, and
(Gen-Inv-P)
.
We apply these rules last, as they are computationally costly, and thus we want to use them once the previous rules have possibly already simplified the state.

5.

As the previous step might have generated new information and added new derived properties to the sets
\mathit{Imp}_{D}
of implied formulas, we reiterate steps 1. – 3. once, in order to take advantage of the new elements of
\mathit{Imp}_{D}
and use them e.g. to substitute further sub-formulas.

6.4.
Discharging Implied Liveness Obligations

Note that the function
\mathit{applyRules}
can potentially discharge non-nested formulas of the form
\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\beta
(that is, substitute them with
\top
) by combining
(Gen-Reach)
and
(Simplify-Non-Nested)
, if they are implied by other parts of the specification.
This allows us to sometimes simplify specifications that contain
\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
to safety ones.
However, this does not work for the common
\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.19 L 0 7.74 L 8.94 7.74 L 8.94 -1.19 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\beta
-pattern as it requires checking the global property that
\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}

.

guarantees that if before the above transformation the monitor satisfied the monitor-state correctness property (

), then after the transformation it does so as well.

6.5.
Construction of the Verdict-Labelling Function

If a state
q
of the monitor is such that
\mathit{Formula}(q)=\bot
, no words that have a prefix reaching
q
can satisfy
\Phi
.
Hence, the monitor can assign verdict
\mathsf{UNSAT}
to state
q
.
For states where
\mathit{Formula}(q)
is not
\bot
, we cannot always assign a verdict.
In general, it is not always possible to determine satisfaction of a formula based on a finite prefix.
However, if
\mathit{Formula}(q)
represents a safety language, the monitor can assign a
\mathsf{SAFETY}
verdict, since expansion is sufficient for tracking safety requirements.
For our verdict-labelling function, we consider a syntactic criterion for safety temporal formulas.

Let

Q_{\bot}:=\{q\in Q\mid\mathit{Formula}(q)=\bot\}
and

Q_{\mathit{safe}}
be the set of all states
q_{s}
such that no
q_{l}\in Q
where
\mathit{Formula}(q_{l})
is not a syntactic-safety formula is reachable.
We define
\mathit{Verdict}
as follows.

\mathit{Verdict}(q):=\begin{cases}\mathsf{UNSAT}&\text{if }q\in Q_{\bot},\\
\mathsf{SAFETY}&\text{if }q\in Q_{\mathit{safe}}\\
\mathsf{OPEN}&\text{otherwise}.\\
\end{cases}

\mathit{Verdict}
is well-defined as for
q\in Q_{\bot}
,
\delta(q,a)\in Q_{\bot}
for any
a\in 2^{\mathcal{P}}
.
Furthermore, any
q\in Q_{\mathit{safe}}
is by definition syntactic-safety.
As expansion preserves syntactic-safety and the rules do not add any non-safety formulas to
E_{D}
or
F_{D}
, any successors of
q
is also either syntactic-safety or in
Q_{\bot}
.

Example 6.10.

In
Example

6.6
and
Example

6.8
the states get an
\mathsf{UNSAT}
and
\mathsf{SAFETY}
verdict, respectively, after applying the rules.
Without the rules they would both get an
\mathsf{OPEN}
verdict.

Theorem 6.11.

Let
M
be a monitor constructed from
\Phi\in\mathit{RP{-}LTL}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}\cup\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
as described in
Section

6
.
Then,
M
satisfies the conditions in
Definition

5.2
and is hence a monitor for
\Phi
.

7.
Experimental Evaluation

7.1.
The Logic
TSL-MT
and Reactive Program Games

In our implementation, we use Temporal Stream Logic modulo Theories (TSL-MT)
(
Finkbeiner et al., 2019
;
Finkbeiner et al., 2022a
)
and reactive program games (RPGs)
(
Heim and Dimitrova, 2024
)
instead of
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
and the symbolic games, respectively.
Similar to
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
, TSL-MT allows us to specify temporal properties over inputs
\mathbb{I}
and program variables
\mathbb{X}
(often called cells, and denoted by
\mathbb{C}
).
The only difference is that instead of generic predicates over the next-step program variables
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
, TSL-MT is restricted to so called
updates
.
Intuitively, updates behave like program variable assignments.
Denoted as
\llbracket x\leftarrowtail t\rrbracket
, an update assigns the value of the term
t
(which might include program variables and inputs) to
x
for the next state.
Different updates of the same variable are mutually exclusive at the same time.
Analogously, RPGs allow the system player to only select updates.
This restriction makes extracting programs easier, as the system is restricted to a finite number of known assignments.
TSL-MT and RPGs can be encoded in the more general framework of
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
and symbolic games such that our results apply.
Furthermore, we believe that the game-solving acceleration technique from
(
Heim and Dimitrova, 2024
)
could be lifted to our symbolic games.
Here we consider TSL-MT and RPGs, as for those tools and benchmarks exist.

7.2.
Prototype Tool Implementation

We implemented our approach in our prototype tool
tslmt2rpg

1
1

1

Available at
https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.13939202
.
.
The generation of the monitor is done on-the-fly while constructing the product.
This allows us to only consider predicate selections actually appearing in the game and to only explore states that matter for the game.
For
\mathit{PropPreds}
we use the predicates that appear in the specification, the negations, and equalities for updates with constants.
We disable the
(Gen-Inv-P)
rule by default, as computing exact fixpoints is time-consuming, but often not needed.
For the automata constructions we use Spot
(
Duret-Lutz et al., 2022
)
.
As SMT and CHC solver we use
z3

(
de Moura and Bjørner, 2008
)
.
For the rules
(Gen-Reach)
and
(Gen-Inv-P)
we use the
\mu
CLP solver
MuVal

(
Unno et al., 2023
)
and the Optimal CHC solver
OptPCSat

(
Gu et al., 2023
)
, respectively.
For solving the generated reactive program game we use
rpgsolve

(
Heim and Dimitrova, 2024
)
.

7.3.
Benchmarks

In the following, we discuss the content, purpose, and results of our benchmark sets
2
2

2

All benchmarks are available at
https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.13939202
.
.
Note that we restrict our benchmarks to integers, since reals cannot be handled by some of the tools and solvers we compare to, although
tslmt2rpg
also supports reals.
We begin with the description of the benchmarks we used in the evaluation, grouped into four categories listed below.

Benchmarks from the Literature

We evaluated
tslmt2rpg
on the existing TSL-MT benchmarks from
(
Neider and Topcu, 2016
)
and
(
Maderbacher and Bloem, 2022
)
.
We do not include benchmarks from
(
Choi et al., 2022
)
as some contain uninterpreted functions and others are trivially realizable with a system violating the environment assumptions.

Basic Properties

The second set contains benchmarks with simple reachability properties and invariants implied by or contradicted by some of the other invariants.
They do not need complicated strategic decisions and can be handled mainly on the language level.

Scenarios

We created a set of more intriguing scenario benchmarks.
This includes cyber-physical-system controllers and robot mission planning tasks.
They are larger than the previous benchmarks and need more complex reasoning by the game solver.
They mostly require strategic decisions at some crucial points and are fairly deterministic in-between.
This is common for specifications of more complex systems.

Limitations

With the last set of benchmarks we analyze the limitations of our monitor-based method.
These are benchmarks were a language-level analysis cannot effectively prune the game, the
(Gen-Inv-P)
rule is actually needed, or fixpoints in the different rules are too difficult to compute.

TO

We compare our approach of pruning the symbolic game with a monitor to the naive generation and direct solving workflow (which we also implement in
tslmt2rpg
).
In addition, we compare to the TSL-MT synthesis tools
Raboniel
(
Maderbacher and Bloem, 2022
)
and
TeMoS
(
Choi et al., 2022
)
which are both based on abstraction refinement and LTL synthesis using
Strix
(
Meyer et al., 2018
)
.

Table

1
shows the results of our evaluation, starting with the examples from
Section

2
, followed by the four categories of benchmarks described above.

Benchmarks from the Literature

The benchmarks from the literature can already be handled by existing tools.
We can see that constructing the product with the monitor creates an expectable overhead during the computation.
However, solving them is still possible with the monitor.

Basic Properties

The monitor can easily dismiss the basic properties described in this benchmark set.
However, the other approaches usually cannot handle those properties, and if they can, this is because some very local reasoning is possible.
This shows that our monitors are indeed a useful enhancement that can lead to improved performance.

Scenarios

As shown by the empirical results, the monitor product outperforms the other approaches on our scenario benchmarks.
The reasons is that the rules greatly simplify the reasoning in the periods in-between the decision making points in those scenarios and make some bad decisions obvious.
This shows that the monitor product indeed has its merits for more complex specifications.

Limitations

By design of these benchmarks, our approach does not perform well on them.
However, our experiments show that these benchmarks are challenging and cannot be handled by other techniques either.

8.
Related Work

Synthesis of Infinite-State Reactive Systems

The work on synthesis of reactive systems from temporal logic specifications extended with richer data domains has focused on the logics TSL
(
Finkbeiner et al., 2019
)
and
TSL-MT

(
Finkbeiner et al., 2022a
)
, and the logic LTL
T

(
Rodríguez and Sánchez, 2023
)
.
The synthesis techniques in
(
Finkbeiner et al., 2019
;
Choi et al., 2022
;
Maderbacher and Bloem, 2022
)
are based on propositional abstraction and iterative refinement of the specification by introducing assumptions.
The logic LTL
T
considered in
(
Rodríguez and Sánchez, 2023
)
restricts the atomic propositions to be literals over
current-time-step
variables only. This enables the method in
(
Rodríguez and Sánchez, 2023
)
to encode the theory specification into an equirealizable Boolean LTL formula. Thus, LTL
T
realizability is decidable under decidability assumptions for the underlying first-order theory. In contrast,
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
allows the specification of relationships between current and next-state variables, and the realizability and synthesis problems are undecidable.
All of the above approaches rely on a procedure for finite-state synthesis to solve the resulting LTL synthesis task. Our method, on the other hand, treats the
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
specification directly by constructing an infinite-state two-player game encoding the synthesis task.

Another line of work on synthesis of reactive systems with unbounded data domains focuses on solving infinite-state games that directly encode the synthesis problem.
Abstraction-based methods
(
Walker and Ryzhyk, 2014
;
Vechev et al., 2013
;
Grumberg et al., 2007
;
Henzinger et al., 2003
;
Finkbeiner et al., 2022b
;
Azzopardi et al., 2023
)
extend techniques such as abstract interpretation and counterexample-guided refinelment to two-player games.
Symbolic game-solving techniques work directly with the infinite-state game.
This class of techniques includes constraint-based approaches
(
Faella and Parlato, 2023
;
Farzan and Kincaid, 2018
;
Katis et al., 2018
)
for special classes of wining conditions and symbolic fixpoint computation methods
(
Samuel et al., 2021
;
Samuel et al., 2023
;
Heim and Dimitrova, 2024
;
Schmuck et al., 2024
)
.

The approach we propose in this paper is agnostic to the game solver and can enhance different methods for solving symbolic games.

Automata Constructions and Satisfiability Checking for LTL

The construction of alternating Büchi word automata (ABW) from LTL
(
Vardi, 1994
;
Vardi, 1995
)
is the first where the transition function is defined by following the LTL expansion laws.
The algorithm in
(
Gerth et al., 1995
)
uses tableaux, based on the LTL expansion laws, to translate LTL to generalized nondeterministic Büchi automata (GNBA). Both ABW and GNBA can be translated to nondeterministic Büchi automata, which can subsequently be determinized
(
Safra, 1988
;
Piterman, 2007
)
to obtain a DPA. In this two-step construction of DPA from LTL, the semantic structure of the states of the automaton and their correspondence to the LTL formula is lost. This limitation has motivated a line of work
(
Kretínský and Esparza, 2012
;
Esparza et al., 2020
;
Esparza et al., 2022
)
, which developed methods for direct translation of LTL to deterministic
\omega
-automata.
Similarly to the tableaux-based constructions, this translation uses the LTL expansion laws. This enables semantics-based reductions, such as merging states representing equivalent formulas, and heuristics for synthesis
(
Luttenberger et al., 2020
)
.

Tableaux techniques for checking LTL satisfiability were first studied by Wolper
(
Wolper, 1985
)
and subsequently developed by
(
Schwendimann, 1998
;
Reynolds, 2016
;
Bertello et al., 2016
)
.
Several different approaches to LTL satisfiability have been proposed.
Methods based on model checking
(
Rozier and Vardi, 2010
)
reduce the problem to the verification of the negated formula against a universal model.
Some recent techniques employ SAT solvers
(
Li et al., 2013
;
Li et al., 2014
;
Geatti et al., 2024b
)
, taking advantage of the progress in SAT solving.
Recently,
(
Hermo et al., 2023
)
proposed a tableaux method for checking the realizability of the safety fragment of LTL.

None of these techniques handle the non-Boolean variable domains that our method tackles.

Satisfiability Checking and Automata Constructions for First-Order Temporal Logics

The decidability of the satisfiability problem for fragments of first-order linear and branching-time temporal logics has been extensively studied
(
Hodkinson et al., 2001
;
Hodkinson et al., 2002
;
Hodkinson et al., 2003
)
.

(
Demri, 2006
;
Demri and D’Souza, 2007
)
study LTL with constraints,
which is a fragment of first-order LTL. In general, the satisfiability problem for these logics is undecidable.
The model-checking problem for an extension of LTL with atomic propositions over variables with possibly infinite domains has been considered in
(
Grumberg et al., 2012
)
.
(
Faran and Kupferman, 2018
)
introduces linear temporal logic with arithmetic (LTLA), which extends LTL with linear arithmetic over the integers.

(
Faran and Kupferman, 2020
)
studies the synthesis problem for specifications in the form of variable automata with arithmetic.

Symbolic automata
(
D’Antoni and Veanes, 2015
;
D’Antoni and Veanes, 2021
)
extend finite-state automata with support for potentially infinite alphabets represented by effective Boolean algebras.
They were recently generalized to
\omega
-regular languages of infinite words in
(
Veanes et al., 2023
)
, which introduces alternating and nondeterministic Büchi automata modulo
\mathcal{A}
(
ABW
A
and
NBW
A
, respectively), where
\mathcal{A}
is an effective Boolean algebra. Further,
(
Veanes et al., 2023
)
provides algorithms for the construction of
ABW
A
from specifications in LTL modulo
\mathcal{A}
, as well as in the combination of LTL modulo
\mathcal{A}
with extended regular expressions modulo
\mathcal{A}
.
The key idea is the use of symbolic transition terms and symbolic derivatives, which enable symbolic automata constructions integrating theory reasoning and rewriting. The translation of
ABW
A
into equivalent

NBW
A
is also performed using symbolic derivatives, resulting in a symbolic generalization of the classical alternation elimination algorithm
(
Miyano and Hayashi, 1984
)
.
Our approach, on the other hand, targets the construction of deterministic monitors, as determinism is necessary for building the game encoding the synthesis problem. Investigating the use of symbolic derivatives to construct a deterministic transition relation in our context is a possible avenue for future work, with the potential to improve the efficiency of the successor-state computation.
Finally, we focus on
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
specifications, while extensions of temporal logics with regular expressions have proven useful for practical adoption in verification
(
Standards, 2010
)
. The logic
RLTL
A

introduced in
(
Veanes et al., 2023
)
, combines LTL modulo
\mathcal{A}
with
extended regular expressions modulo
\mathcal{A}
. The combination is facilitated by symbolic transition terms, which provide a common semantics.

(
Geatti et al., 2024a
)
considers finite-word semantics of first-order LTL and proposes an automaton model and a corresponding notion of regular first-order languages. They study the closure of these languages under common operations and establish conditions under which non-emptiness is semi-decidable. Our work, on the other hand, focuses on infinite-word semantics.

Monitoring LTL and First-Order LTL

Monitors for LTL are also essential in the context of runtime verification.
Their construction typically uses the LTL expansion laws
(
Havelund and Rosu, 2001
;
Sen et al., 2003
)
.
Recent work
(
Havelund and Peled, 2021
;
Havelund and Peled, 2018
)
has developed theory and tools for monitoring first-order temporal logic, focusing on safety.

None of these constructions targets applications in synthesis or can perform the temporal and first-order reasoning crucial for our transformation rules.

9.
Conclusion

A common approach to synthesizing infinite-state reactive programs out of temporal logic specifications is to turn the specification into a symbolic game, which is then solved.
However, a shortcoming of existing approaches is that the construction of the symbolic game loses semantic information from the high-level specification, making the game hard to solve.
For example, while it is easy to see that the formula
x=0\land(\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.19 L 0 7.74 L 8.94 7.74 L 8.94 -1.19 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}x^{\prime}>x)\land(\operatorname{\hbox to8.7pt{\vbox to8.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.76527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 L 5.66 9.23 L 11.32 3.57 L 5.66 -2.09 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}x<0)
is unrealizable, it is less evident from the constructed symbolic game.

In this paper, we introduce monitors, which provide the basis of a flexible framework for systematically adding some of the missing semantic information to the game.
The monitors are constructed from the temporal logic formula via rules combining first-order and temporal reasoning.
We use these monitors to prune the synthesis game’s state space and winning condition by constructing a product game from the game and the monitor.
Our method is general because it is independent of the game construction and game solving.
It is extensible, allowing for the easy addition of new rules or the complete swap out of the monitor construction.
We demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach empirically.

We believe our method and monitor are general enough that, for future work, they could be extended to more expressive formalisms, like regular expressions modulo theories.
Furthermore, while the independence of the game construction is an advantage of our approach, a deeper integration of our method and the game construction – like done in the construction of symbolic automata – might yield even better-pruned games.
Another possible extension is to find a way to extend the monitor construction with some form of inexpensive strategic reasoning.

Acknowledgements.

We thank the anonymous reviewers for their helpful suggestions.

Appendix A
Proofs

A.1.
Proofs from
Section

4
and
Section

5

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
be the symbolic game with
\mathcal{G}=(L,l_{\mathit{init}},\mathbb{I},\mathbb{X},\mathit{dom},\delta)
.
For a
\rho\in{(\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}))}^{\omega}
we define
\mathit{uniqueRun}(\rho,\mathcal{G})\in\mathcal{S}^{\omega}
as the unique run in
\llbracket\mathcal{G}\rrbracket
that starts in
l_{\mathit{init}}
and conforms to all assignments to
\mathbb{I}
and
\mathbb{X}
.
The run is unique by the first condition
Definition

4.5
.
We define the
pseudo-language
of
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
as
\mathcal{L}_{P}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda):=\{\rho\in{(\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}))}^{\omega}\mid\mathit{uniqueRun}(\rho,\mathcal{G})\in\llbracket\Lambda\rrbracket\}
.

Let
\varphi
be an
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}(specvars)
formula, and
\widehat{\varphi}
be its propositional version.

For each
\rho\in{(\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}))}^{\omega}
, there exists a unique sequence
\widehat{\rho}\in{(2^{\mathit{AP}(\widehat{\varphi})})}^{\omega}
such that for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, and all
p\in\mathit{AP}(\widehat{\varphi})
,

\rho[i]\models p
if and only if
p\in\widehat{\rho}[i]
.

By structural induction on the definition of
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
, it is easy to show that

\rho\models\varphi\Longleftrightarrow\widehat{\rho}\models\widehat{\varphi}.

For each
\eta\in{(2^{\mathit{AP}(\widehat{\varphi})})}^{\omega}
, we define

\mathit{concretize}(\eta):=\{\rho\in{(\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}))}^{\omega}\mid\widehat{\rho}=\eta\}.

By structural induction on the definition of
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
, it is easy to show that

\eta\models\widehat{\varphi}\Longrightarrow\mathit{concretize}(\eta)\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\varphi).

Lemma A.1.

Let
\varphi\in\mathit{RP{-}LTL}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}\cup\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
and
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
be
a symbolic game for
\varphi
, then

\mathcal{L}(\varphi)=\mathcal{L}_{P}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda).

Proof.

Since
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
is a symbolic game for
\varphi
, there exists a DPA
\mathcal{A}_{\widehat{\varphi}}
from which
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
was constructed. We prove the two inclusions separately.

(\subseteq)\quad

Let
\rho\in\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
.
Then,
\widehat{\rho}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{\widehat{\varphi}})
.
Since
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
is constructed from
\mathcal{A}_{\widehat{\varphi}}
, we have that
\mathit{concretize}(\widehat{\rho})\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{P}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
.
Thus,
\rho\in\mathcal{L}_{P}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
.

(\supseteq)\quad

Let
\rho\in\mathcal{L}_{P}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
.
Then, since
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
is constructed from
\mathcal{A}_{\widehat{\varphi}}
, we have that
\widehat{\rho}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{\widehat{\varphi}})
.
Therefore,
\mathit{concretize}(\widehat{\rho})\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
.
Thus,
\rho\in\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
.
∎

Lemma A.2.

Let

\varphi\in\mathit{RP{-}LTL}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}\cup\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
and

(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
be
some symbolic game
where
\mathcal{G}=(L,l_{\mathit{init}},\mathbb{I},\mathbb{X},\mathit{dom},\delta)

such that

\mathcal{L}(\varphi)=\mathcal{L}_{P}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
.
Then
\varphi
is realizable if and only if
(l_{\mathit{init}},\mathbf{x})\in\mathit{Win}_{\mathit{Sys}}(\llbracket\mathcal{G}\rrbracket,\llbracket\Lambda\rrbracket)
for every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X})
.

Proof.

We prove separately the two directions.

(\Longrightarrow)\quad

Let
\varphi
be realizable.
Then, there exists a function

\sigma:\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X})\times\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{I})^{+}\to\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X})
such that for every infinite sequence
\mathit{input}\in\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{I})^{\omega}
of assignments to
\mathbb{I}
and initial assignment
\mathit{init}\in\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X})
, for
\rho\in{(\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}))}^{\omega}
defined as

\rho[0]:=\mathit{init}\cup\mathit{input}[0]
and
\rho[n]:=\sigma(\mathit{init},\mathit{input}[0,n-1])\cup\mathit{input}[n]
for
n>0
,
\rho\models\varphi
holds.

We can define a strategy
\sigma_{\mathit{Sys}}
for Player
\mathit{Sys}
in
\llbracket\mathcal{G}\rrbracket
that emulates the function
\sigma
.
Then, every
\xi\in\mathit{Plays}_{\llbracket\mathcal{G}\rrbracket}((l_{\mathit{init}},\mathbf{x}),\sigma_{\mathit{Sys}})
corresponds to a sequence
\rho\in{(\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}))}^{\omega}
consistent with the function
\sigma
.
Thus, since
\mathcal{L}(\varphi)=\mathcal{L}_{P}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
,
we have that
\xi\in\llbracket\Lambda\rrbracket
.
Therefore, we conclude that
\sigma_{\mathit{Sys}}
is a winning strategy for
Player
\mathit{Sys}
from
(l_{\mathit{init}},\mathbf{x})
, and hence

(l_{\mathit{init}},\mathbf{x})\in\mathit{Win}_{\mathit{Sys}}(\llbracket\mathcal{G}\rrbracket,\llbracket\Lambda\rrbracket)
.

(\Longleftarrow)\quad

Suppose that
(l_{\mathit{init}},\mathbf{x})\in\mathit{Win}_{\mathit{Sys}}(\llbracket\mathcal{G}\rrbracket,\llbracket\Lambda\rrbracket)
for every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X})
.
Then, for every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X})
, there exists a winning strategy for Player
\mathit{Sys}
in
\llbracket\mathcal{G}\rrbracket
from
(l_{\mathit{init}},\mathbf{x})
.
We can define a function
\sigma:\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X})\times\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{I})^{+}\to\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X})
that for every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X})
mimics the respective winning strategy for player
\mathit{Sys}
.
Thus, for every infinite sequence
\mathit{input}\in\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{I})^{\omega}
of assignments to
\mathbb{I}
and initial assignment
\mathit{init}\in\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X})
, for
\rho\in{(\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}))}^{\omega}
defined as

\rho[0]:=\mathit{init}\cup\mathit{input}[0]
and
\rho[n]:=\sigma(\mathit{init},\mathit{input}[0,n-1])\cup\mathit{input}[n]
for
n>0
, we have that
\rho
corresponds to a play consistent with the respective winning strategy for player
\mathit{Sys}
from
\mathbf{x}
.
Since
\mathcal{L}(\varphi)=\mathcal{L}_{P}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
, we have
\rho\models\varphi
.
∎

See
4.8

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of
Lemma

A.1
and
Lemma

A.2
.
The condition
\mathbf{x}\models_{T}\mathit{dom}(l_{\mathit{init}})
is not relevant as our construction sets
\mathit{dom}
always to true.
∎

See
5.4

Proof.

Since
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
is a symbolic game for
\varphi
, we have that
\mathit{dom}(l)=\top
of all
l\in L
.
By the definition of
(\mathcal{G}_{\times},\Lambda_{\times})
,

\mathit{dom}_{\times}(l,q):=\mathit{dom}(l)=\top
for all
(l,q)
.
This, the condition
\mathbf{x}\models_{T}\mathit{dom}((l_{\mathit{init}},q_{\mathit{init}}))
is not relevant.
We will show that

\mathcal{L}_{P}(\mathcal{G}_{\times},\Lambda_{\times})=\mathcal{L}_{P}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
,
and the desired claim will directly follow from
Lemma

A.1
and
Lemma

A.2
.
We show the two inclusions separately.

(\subseteq)\quad

Let
\rho\in\mathcal{L}_{P}(\mathcal{G}_{\times},\Lambda_{\times})
.
Thus,
\mathit{uniqueRun}(\rho,\mathcal{G}_{\times})\in\llbracket\Lambda_{\times}\rrbracket
.
The projection of
\mathit{uniqueRun}(\rho,\mathcal{G}_{\times})
on
L
is equal to
\mathit{uniqueRun}(\rho,\mathcal{G})
.
If
\mathit{uniqueRun}(\rho,\mathcal{G})\in\llbracket\Lambda\rrbracket
, then we are done.
Suppose that
\mathit{uniqueRun}(\rho,\mathcal{G})\not\in\llbracket\Lambda\rrbracket
.
Let
\mathit{uniqueRun}(\rho,\mathcal{G}_{\times})=(l_{0},q_{0})(l_{1},q_{1})\ldots\in\Lambda_{\times}
.
By definition of
\Lambda_{\times}
,

\mathit{Verdict}(q_{i})\neq\mathsf{UNSAT}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, and
there exists
i\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathit{Verdict}(q_{i})=\mathsf{SAFETY}
.
By
Definition

5.2
, the second condition, we have that
\rho\in\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
.
Since
(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
is a symbolic game for
\varphi
, we have by
Lemma

A.1
that

\mathcal{L}(\varphi)=\mathcal{L}_{P}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
.
Thus,
\rho\in\mathcal{L}_{P}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
, which contradicts our supposition that
\mathit{uniqueRun}(\rho,\mathcal{G})\not\in\llbracket\Lambda\rrbracket
.
This concludes the proof in this direction.

(\supseteq)\quad
Let
\rho\in\mathcal{L}_{P}(\mathcal{G},\Lambda)
.
Thus,
\mathit{uniqueRun}(\rho,\mathcal{G})\in\llbracket\Lambda\rrbracket
.
Consider
\mathit{uniqueRun}(\rho,\mathcal{G}_{\times})
.
The projection of
\mathit{uniqueRun}(\rho,\mathcal{G}_{\times})
on
L
is equal to
\mathit{uniqueRun}(\rho,\mathcal{G})
.
Thus, by the definition of
\Lambda_{\times}
, since
\mathit{uniqueRun}(\rho,\mathcal{G})\in\llbracket\Lambda\rrbracket
,
we also have
\mathit{uniqueRun}(\rho,\mathcal{G}_{\times})\in\Lambda_{\times}
.
∎

A.2.
Proofs from
Section

6

See
6.2

Proof.

The proofs follows a standard argument via induction on
\varphi

(
Esparza et al., 2020
)
.

•

\varphi=\alpha
:

\rho\models\varphi\Longleftrightarrow\langle\rho[0],\rho[1]\rangle\models_{T}\alpha\Longleftrightarrow\mathit{expand}(\alpha,a)=\top\Longleftrightarrow\rho_{+1}\models\mathit{expand}(\varphi,a)

•

\varphi=\neg\psi
:

\rho\models\neg\psi\Longleftrightarrow\rho\not\models\psi\Longleftrightarrow\rho_{+1}\models\neg\mathit{expand}(\psi,a)\Longleftrightarrow\rho_{+1}\models\mathit{expand}(\varphi,a)

•

\varphi=\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2}
:

\begin{array}[]{l}\rho\models\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2}\Longleftrightarrow\\
\rho\models\varphi_{1}\text{ and }\rho\models\varphi_{2}\Longleftrightarrow\\
\rho_{+1}\models\mathit{expand}(\varphi_{1},a)\text{ and }\rho_{+1}\models\mathit{expand}(\varphi_{2},a)\Longleftrightarrow\\
\rho_{+1}\models\mathit{expand}(\varphi,a)\end{array}

•

\varphi=\operatorname{\hbox to7.41pt{\vbox to7.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.70276pt\lower-1.11943pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.57 M 4.77 3.57 C 4.77 6.21 2.63 8.34 0 8.34 C -2.63 8.34 -4.77 6.21 -4.77 3.57 C -4.77 0.94 -2.63 -1.19 0 -1.19 C 2.63 -1.19 4.77 0.94 4.77 3.57 Z M 0 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\psi
:

\rho\models\operatorname{\hbox to7.41pt{\vbox to7.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.70276pt\lower-1.11943pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {

\lxSVG@closescope }

•

\varphi=\varphi_{1}\LTLu\varphi_{2}
:

\begin{array}[]{l}\rho\models\varphi_{1}\LTLu\varphi_{2}\Longleftrightarrow\\
\rho\models\varphi_{2}\vee(\varphi_{1}\land\operatorname{\hbox to7.41pt{\vbox to7.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.70276pt\lower-1.11943pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {

\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\varphi)\Longleftrightarrow\\
\rho_{+1}\models\mathit{expand}(\varphi_{2},a)\vee\mathit{expand}(\varphi_{1},a)\land(\varphi_{1}\LTLu\varphi_{2})\Longleftrightarrow\\
\rho_{+1}\models\mathit{expand}(\varphi,a)\end{array}

∎

Lemma A.3 (Lift
Lemma

6.2
).

For all
\rho\in{(\mathit{Assignments}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}))}^{\omega}
and
a\subseteq\mathcal{P}
where

\langle\rho[0],\rho[1]\rangle\models_{T}\left(\bigwedge_{\alpha\in\mathcal{P}\cap a}\alpha\right)\land\left(\bigwedge_{\alpha\in\mathcal{P}\setminus a}\lnot\alpha\right)
,
it holds that

\rho\models\mathit{Formula}(q)
if and only if
\rho_{+1}\models\mathit{Formula}(\mathit{nextState}(q,a))
.

Proof.

This follows from
Lemma

6.2
and the fact that
\mathit{nextState}
applies
\mathit{expand}
point-wise to the elements of
q
.
This corresponds to applying
\mathit{expand}
to the overall
\mathit{Formula}(q)
.

The only difference is the use of
\mathit{propagate}
.
However,
\mathit{propagate}
only contains predicates that are trivially implied by
a
, and we only consider
\rho
where
a
holds initially.
Therefore, the elements added to
\mathit{nextState}
by
\mathit{propagate}
also hold for
\rho_{+1}
.
∎

Lemma A.4.

Let
\textsc{Transform}:Q\to Q
be a monitor-state transformation function and consider

q_{1}=\langle F_{\mathsf{A}}^{1},E_{\mathsf{A}}^{1},F_{\mathsf{G}}^{1},E_{\mathsf{G}}^{1},\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}}^{1},\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}^{1}\rangle
and

q_{2}=\langle F_{\mathsf{A}}^{2},E_{\mathsf{A}}^{2},F_{\mathsf{G}}^{2},E_{\mathsf{G}}^{2},\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}}^{2},\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}^{2}\rangle
where

\textsc{Transform}(q_{1})=q_{2}
.

•

If
\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}}^{2}=\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}}^{1}
and
\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}^{2}=\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}^{1}
, then condition (b) in
Definition

6.3
is satisfied.

•

If
F_{D}^{2}=F_{D}^{1}
,
E_{D}^{2}=E_{D}^{1}
and
\mathit{Imp}_{D}^{2}\supseteq\mathit{Imp}_{D}^{1}
, for all
D\in\{\mathsf{A},\mathsf{G}\}
, and if condition (b) in
Definition

6.3
is satisfied, then conditions (a) and (c) in
Definition

6.3
are also satisfied.

•

If
E_{D}^{2}=E_{D}^{1}
, then condition (c) in
Definition

6.3
is satisfied.

We show for each rule in
\mathit{RP{-}LTL}
-
\mathit{Rules}
that the respective monitor-state transformation

Transform
satisfies the conditions of

for all

with

\textsc{Transform}(q)=q^{\prime}
.

(UNSAT)
.

The premise

entails that

, and hence condition (c) is satisfied.

\lxSVG@closescope }

Rule
(Simplify-Non-Nested)
.

The premise of the rule guarantees that

\llparenthesis E_{D}\cup\mathit{Imp}_{D}\rrparenthesis\rightarrow(\gamma\leftrightarrow\top)
.
Furthermore, since
\gamma\in\mathit{QF}(\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{I}\cup\mathbb{X}^{\prime})
, we have that

\llparenthesis F_{D}\rrparenthesis\land\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.19 L 0 7.74 L 8.94 7.74 L 8.94 -1.19 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\gamma\leftrightarrow\top)\equiv\llparenthesis F_{D}\rrparenthesis[\gamma\mapsto\top]\land\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.51666pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -1.19 L 0 7.74 L 8.94 7.74 L 8.94 -1.19 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\gamma\leftrightarrow\top)
.
Since the substitution is performed only in
F_{D}
, we obtain

\llparenthesis F_{\mathsf{G}}\cup E_{\mathsf{G}}\cup\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}\rrparenthesis\equiv\llparenthesis F_{\mathsf{G}}^{\prime}\cup E_{\mathsf{G}}^{\prime}\cup\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}^{\prime}\rrparenthesis
.
The rule does not modify the sets
\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}}
and
\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}
,
thus condition (b) is also satisfied.

Rule
(Propagate-Assump)
.

Since
\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}^{\prime}=\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}}\cup\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}
,
we have that
\llparenthesis F_{\mathsf{G}}\cup E_{\mathsf{G}}\cup\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}\rrparenthesis\equiv\llparenthesis F_{\mathsf{A}}\cup E_{\mathsf{A}}\cup\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}}\rrparenthesis\rightarrow\llparenthesis F_{\mathsf{G}}\cup E_{\mathsf{G}}\cup\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}^{\prime}\rrparenthesis\equiv\llparenthesis F_{\mathsf{G}}^{\prime}\cup E_{\mathsf{G}}^{\prime}\cup\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}^{\prime}\rrparenthesis
.
Additionally,
\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}^{\prime}=\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{A}}\cup\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}
entails condition (b).

Rule
(Propagate-
\operatorname{\hbox to6.98pt{\vbox to6.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.25833pt\lower-1.11945pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {

\lxSVG@closescope }

)
.

Since
\llparenthesis E_{D}\land\bigwedge\mathit{Imp}_{D}\rrparenthesis\rightarrow\llparenthesis\mathit{Imp}_{D}^{\prime}\rrparenthesis

and
\mathit{Imp}_{D}\subseteq\mathit{Imp}_{D}^{\prime}
,
it directly follows that
\llparenthesis F_{\mathsf{G}}\cup E_{\mathsf{G}}\cup\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}\rrparenthesis\equiv\llparenthesis F_{\mathsf{G}}^{\prime}\cup E_{\mathsf{G}}^{\prime}\cup\mathit{Imp}_{\mathsf{G}}^{\prime}\rrparenthesis
and condition (b) is satisfied.
